PHP and WordPress Development · Answer Key

Part 1: Themes

Sessions 1 to 8. Modules 1 to 4: set up a WordPress 7.1 development site with Docker, WP-CLI, debugging and Mailpit, write PHP the WordPress way, learn the content model, the database and hooks, then build the Pages & Co. block theme with theme.json, templates, patterns and style variations, a child theme, and a classic/hybrid theme with the Loop, custom queries and functions.php. Runs on its own as a short course and ends with the Part 1 project and assessment.

Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 1: WordPress for Developers and Your Local Environment

Answers and reasoning for every Session 1 item: the diagnostic, both self-checks, the predict-then-run items, the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 1 12 self-check items Confidential

Start Here · Diagnostic

Not graded. Each reason also says where the skill comes back in this course. PHP's own answers to D1, D2 and D6 are in the diagnostic lab's output below.

D1
What does this PHP print?
$books = ['Dune' => 14.99, '1984' => 9.99, 'Emma' => 7.99];
echo count($books), ' ', array_key_first($books);
A) 3 Dune
B) 3 Emma
C) 2 Dune
D) 3 14.99
Why: count() counts the three entries; array_key_first() returns the first KEY, 'Dune'. WordPress passes arrays of arguments almost everywhere (Lesson 1.3). Skill: PHP arrays. Where it comes back: PHP Foundations Module 3; every WordPress function that takes an array of arguments (Lesson 1.3).
D2
A visitor typed <b>Maya</b> into a form. What reaches the browser from this line?
echo 'Hello, ', htmlspecialchars('<b>Maya</b>');
A) Hello, Maya (in bold)
B) Hello, &lt;b&gt;Maya&lt;/b&gt;
C) Hello, <b>Maya</b>
D) Hello, (the tags are removed)
Why: htmlspecialchars() turns < and > into entities, so the browser shows the tags as text instead of running them. WordPress's esc_html() does the same job. Skill: escaping output. Where it comes back: PHP Foundations Lesson 4.2; esc_html() and friends in Lessons 1.3 and 5.3.
D3
Which query lists the titles of the books under $10, cheapest first?
A) SELECT title FROM books ORDER BY price < 10;
B) SELECT title WHERE price < 10 FROM books;
C) SELECT title FROM books WHERE price < 10 ORDER BY price;
D) SELECT * FROM books HAVING price < 10 DESC;
Why: FROM names the table, WHERE keeps the rows that match, ORDER BY sorts (ascending unless you say DESC). Lesson 2.2 runs queries like this on WordPress's own tables. Skill: SQL. Where it comes back: MySQL Foundations; WordPress's tables in Lesson 2.2 and $wpdb in Lesson 5.5.
D4
Your terminal is in your home folder; the course project is ~/pagesco-dev. Which starts the site?
A) docker compose up -d
B) docker compose up -d pagesco-dev
C) sudo docker compose up -d
D) cd pagesco-dev, then docker compose up -d
Why: Docker Compose reads the compose.yaml of the folder you are in (or a folder above it). From your home folder there is none, so every course command starts with being in the project folder. Skill: folders and the terminal. Where it comes back: Lesson 1.2 (every docker compose command) and every lab.
D5
A browser asks a server for a page that does not exist. Which status code should come back?
A) 404 Not Found
B) 200 OK
C) 301 Moved Permanently
D) 500 Internal Server Error
Why: 404 means "no such page". 200 is success, 301 a redirect, 500 a crash on the server. WordPress answers 404 when the main query finds nothing, and picks the theme's 404 template. Skill: HTTP status codes. Where it comes back: PHP Foundations Lesson 1.1; rewrite rules and 404 templates in Lessons 2.2 and 3.1.
D6
What does this PHP print?
function shout($t) { return strtoupper($t) . '!'; }
echo implode(' ', array_map('shout', ['Dune', 'Emma']));
A) Dune! Emma!
B) DUNE! EMMA!
C) shout shout
D) An error: a function name cannot be a string
Why: array_map() calls the function whose NAME you pass, once per item. That is a callback. In WordPress you hand function names to add_action() and add_filter(), and core calls them at the right moment (Lesson 2.3). Skill: callbacks. Where it comes back: PHP Foundations functions; hooks in Lesson 2.3 work exactly like this.

1.1 · WordPress for Developers: self-check

Q1
Pages & Co. updates WordPress from 7.1.3 to 7.1.4. Which of these folders does the update REPLACE?
A) wp-content/plugins/pagesco-books/
B) wp-content/themes/pagesco/
C) wp-includes/
D) wp-content/uploads/
Why: wp-includes/, wp-admin/ and the wp-*.php files in the root are core: every update overwrites them, which is why a change there disappears (Lesson 1.1, "Never edit core files"). wp-content/ belongs to the site.
A is wrong: Your own plugin is never touched by a core update.
B is wrong: Your own theme is never touched by a core update (a directory theme such as Twenty Twenty-Five is replaced by its own updates).
D is wrong: Uploads are the site's media; no update touches them.
Q2
From the WP-CLI container, on a fresh course site: what does this print?
curl -s http://localhost:8080/ | grep -o '<title>.*</title>'
<title>Pages &amp; Co.</title>
Why: The home page shows the latest posts, so WordPress titles it with the site title alone; a single post adds its own title and the separator (Hello world! &#8211; Pages &amp; Co.). The title comes from the database, stored escaped: &amp; is the ampersand.
Q3
A front-end request travels through core. Which of these happens FIRST?
A) template-loader.php picks a template from the theme
B) wp() parses the URL and runs the main query
C) the init action fires, after plugins and the theme are loaded
D) the theme's template prints the HTML
Why: wp-settings.php loads core, must-use plugins, plugins and the theme and fires init; only then does wp() work out the request and run the main query, and template-loader.php draws the page. Load, then query, then template.
A is wrong: Choosing a template needs the main query's result, so it comes last but one.
B is wrong: wp() runs after wp-settings.php has finished, init included.
D is wrong: That is the very end of the trip.
Q4
wp core verify-checksums prints Warning: File doesn't verify against checksum: wp-includes/functions.php. What happened, and what do you do?
A) The download was damaged: reinstall WordPress from scratch
B) WordPress 7.1 changed functions.php: nothing to do
C) A plugin is outdated: update all plugins
D) Someone edited a core file: restore it and put the change in a plugin or theme, on a hook
Why: WordPress knows what every core file must contain. A file that fails the check was changed after installation, here by a "quick fix". The next update would silently undo it; security scanners flag it as a possible hack.
A is wrong: A reinstall would also wipe the evidence of what was changed; restore the one file.
B is wrong: Core files match their checksums in every release; a mismatch is never normal.
C is wrong: Plugins live in wp-content/ and are not part of the core checksums.
Q5
With pretty permalinks, which file does the web server hand /books/dune/, /about/ and almost every other front-end URL?
A) wp-config.php
B) index.php in the WordPress root
C) wp-settings.php
D) A file named after the URL, such as books/dune.php
Why: Any request that does not match a real file goes to index.php; from there WordPress loads itself, matches the URL against its rewrite rules and runs the main query. That replaces the one-file-per-page router of PHP Foundations.
A is wrong: wp-config.php only holds settings; it is loaded on the way, by wp-load.php.
C is wrong: wp-settings.php is loaded later in the chain, never requested directly.
D is wrong: No such files exist: the address is matched to content in the database.
Q6
In PHP Foundations you printed a visitor's input with htmlspecialchars(). Which WordPress function do you use instead when printing text into HTML?
esc_html()
Why: It is Lesson 1.1's bridge table: esc_html() for text, esc_attr() inside attributes, esc_url() for links, wp_kses_post() for HTML that may keep safe tags. Lesson 1.3 practices the family; the fixed debug demo of Lesson 1.2 already used it.

1.1 · WordPress for Developers: predict, then run

P1
Predict the output, then run it.
curl -s http://localhost:8080/sample-page/ | grep -o '<title>.*</title>'
<title>Sample Page &#8211; Pages &amp; Co.</title>
Why: A single page is titled with its own title, the separator (an en dash written as &#8211;) and the site title, which is stored escaped (&amp;). Compare self-check Q2: the home page has the site title alone.
P2
Predict the output, then run it.
wp eval 'echo did_action( "init" ), " ", did_action( "template_redirect" ), "\n";'
1 0
Why: WP-CLI loads all of WordPress, so init has fired once (did_action() counts). But no front-end request was made: wp() and the template loader never ran, so template_redirect fired 0 times. That is the line between loading and drawing a page (Lesson 1.1).

1.2 · Your Local Development Environment: self-check

Q7
You want to throw your site away and start over with a blank WordPress. Which command deletes the database?
A) docker compose stop
B) docker compose down
C) docker compose down -v
D) docker compose restart
Why: The database lives in the db_data volume, and only -v ("and the volumes") deletes it. Afterwards delete wordpress/ too and run the install steps again (Lesson 1.2, Everyday Commands).
A is wrong: stop pauses the containers; everything stays.
B is wrong: down removes the containers but keeps the volume: Exercise 1 proves the tagline survives it.
D is wrong: restart stops and starts the same containers.
Q8
What does this print on the course site, exactly?
wp option get blogname
Pages &amp; Co.
Why: WordPress stores the site title HTML-escaped (--title="Pages & Co." is saved with &amp;). Browsers show a plain &, but code that reads the option gets the escaped form: it matters when you compare strings or put the title in an email.
Q9
wp-content/debug.log says PHP Warning: Undefined variable $pagesco_name in /var/www/html/wp-content/mu-plugins/pagesco-debug-demo.php on line 8. Where is that file on YOUR computer?
A) /var/www/html/wp-content/... on your computer's disk
B) wordpress/wp-content/mu-plugins/pagesco-debug-demo.php in your project folder
C) Inside the db container
D) Nowhere: it only exists while the container runs
Why: ./wordpress:/var/www/html in compose.yaml is a bind mount: your wordpress/ folder IS the WordPress root inside the containers. Every path in an error message translates by swapping /var/www/html for your wordpress folder.
A is wrong: /var/www/html exists only inside the containers.
C is wrong: The database container holds the database, not WordPress's files.
D is wrong: A bind-mounted folder is an ordinary folder on your disk; it outlives every container.
Q10
In a new terminal you type wp core version and get no configuration file provided: not found. What is wrong?
A) You are not in the project folder: Docker Compose found no compose.yaml here or above
B) WordPress is not installed yet
C) Docker is not running
D) The wp shortcut is missing
Why: Docker Compose looks for compose.yaml in the current folder and every folder above it. cd ~/pagesco-dev (or any folder inside it) and try again (Lesson 1.2, Common Mistakes 5).
B is wrong: An uninstalled site gives a WP-CLI error about the database tables, not this.
C is wrong: With Docker stopped the message is about the Docker daemon.
D is wrong: Without the shortcut the shell says wp: command not found.
Q11
Most Docker tutorials map 8080:80. Why does the course project use 8080:8080 (Apache listens on 8080 inside the container too)?
A) Port 80 is reserved for MySQL
B) Docker Desktop cannot publish port 80
C) It makes the site faster
D) WordPress calls its own address, http://localhost:8080, from inside the container (WP-Cron, Site Health), and that must reach Apache
Why: With 8080:80 the site works in your browser, but WordPress's requests to itself at http://localhost:8080 reach nothing inside the container, and wp cron test fails. config/apache-8080.conf adds the second port.
A is wrong: MySQL uses 3306, and only on the private network.
B is wrong: It can; the choice is about WordPress's own requests.
C is wrong: Speed is the same; correctness is the point.
Q12
What does this print on the course site? Name one thing that value changes.
wp eval 'echo wp_get_environment_type(), "\n";'
local: for example, Mailpit's mu-plugin only reroutes email when the environment is local; application passwords work over plain HTTP; WordPress 7.1 turns pings off
Why: WP_ENVIRONMENT_TYPE comes from WORDPRESS_CONFIG_EXTRA in compose.yaml. Code (yours included) can behave differently on local, staging and production; Lesson 8.4 sets production.

1.2 · Your Local Development Environment: predict, then run

P3
Predict the output, then run it.
wp db query "SELECT option_value FROM wp_options WHERE option_name = 'blogname'"
option_value
Pages &amp; Co.
Why: wp db query prints the column name and then the value, tab-separated, without a table (in a terminal or not). The value is the escaped title as it sits in the database: the escaping happened when the option was SAVED.
P4
Predict the output, then run it.
wp eval 'var_dump( wp_mail( "", "No address", "Hello" ) );'
bool(false)
Why: No message at all: wp_mail() returns false and WordPress keeps the reason for the wp_mail_failed action (Lesson 1.2, Common Mistakes 3 shows how to listen to it). A silent false is the hardest kind of bug: check return values.

Lab Reference Solutions

Every output below is real: the lab files run exactly as labs/check_labs.py runs them, on a fresh course site (WordPress 7.1.3, PHP 8.5.12, MySQL 9.7, WP-CLI 2.12.0), copied into the WordPress root and run with wp eval-file --use-include (PHP labs) or sh in the WP-CLI container (shell labs). Paths show /var/www/html/..., which is the learner's wordpress/ folder.

Environment check: labs/module-01/l1_0-orientation/check_environment.sh

A tool, not an exercise. On a ready machine it prints (here in a throwaway copy of the course project; on a learner's machine the Docker and Compose versions differ, and on macOS the owner line is left out):

PHP and WordPress Development: environment check
OK    Docker 29.6.2
OK    Docker Compose 5.3.1
OK    project folder pagesco-dev (compose.yaml of project pagesco)
OK    services running: db, mailpit, wordpress
OK    wordpress/ belongs to you (uid 1000): your editor can save in wp-content/
OK    WP-CLI 2.12.0 (the course's wpcli image)
OK    WordPress 7.1.3
OK    PHP 8.5.12 (web server), 8.5.12 (WP-CLI)
OK    site address http://localhost:8080
OK    pretty permalinks /%postname%/
OK    the home page answers 200 inside the containers
OK    WordPress can call itself (wp cron test)
OK    debugging on (WP_DEBUG, WP_DEBUG_LOG, WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY), environment local, wp-content writable
OK    wp_mail() sent a test message and Mailpit caught it
OK    this computer reaches http://localhost:8080/ (the site) and http://localhost:8025/ (Mailpit)
READY for PHP and WordPress Development

On PHP 8.4 the PHP line says CHECK and the verdict is READY WITH NOTES; a project under /mnt/c on Windows adds a CHECK line too. The FIX lines name what to do; the most common are "Docker is installed but not running", "not running: db mailpit wordpress" (docker compose up -d), the Linux file owner, "WordPress is not installed yet" and the missing Mailpit plugin. --part2 adds Node 24, the composer:2 image and the starting site of Part 2 (pagesco active after Lesson 4.3): Session 9 uses it.

Diagnostic practice: labs/module-01/l1_0-orientation/diagnostic_starter.php → diagnostic_solution.php

As shipped the starter prints PHP's answers with the three gaps still in place (the last key instead of the first, no escaping, the wrong callback):

D1  PHP says: 3 Emma
D2  PHP says: Hello, <b>Maya</b>
D6  PHP says: dune emma

The solution prints:

D1  PHP says: 3 Dune
D2  PHP says: Hello, &lt;b&gt;Maya&lt;/b&gt;
D6  PHP says: DUNE! EMMA!

Common sticking points: htmlspecialchars typed inside the quotes (it prints the word); 'pagesco_shout()' with parentheses as the callback (a callback is the NAME); D2's output looks wrong in the terminal and right in a browser, which is the point of escaping.

Lab 1.1-A (guided): labs/module-01/l1_1-wordpress-for-developers/core_tour_starter.sh → core_tour_solution.sh

As shipped:

1. wp-load.php loads your settings file:
50:	require_once ABSPATH . 'wp-config.php';
2. wp-settings.php runs every must-use plugin:
TODO 2
3. wp-settings.php fires plugins_loaded, then init:
TODO 3
4. template-loader.php: the last chance to redirect, then the filter that can swap the template:
TODO 4
5. PHP files in wp-includes, the core library:
TODO 5

The solution prints:

1. wp-load.php loads your settings file:
50:	require_once ABSPATH . 'wp-config.php';
2. wp-settings.php runs every must-use plugin:
506:foreach ( wp_get_mu_plugins() as $mu_plugin ) {
3. wp-settings.php fires plugins_loaded, then init:
630:do_action( 'plugins_loaded' );
779:do_action( 'init' );
4. template-loader.php: the last chance to redirect, then the filter that can swap the template:
23:	do_action( 'template_redirect' );
114:	$template   = apply_filters( 'template_include', $template );
5. PHP files in wp-includes, the core library:
1059

What each step shows: (1) wp-load.php finds your settings; (2) must-use plugins run first, from a plain foreach, no activation; (3) plugins are loaded and plugins_loaded fires, then the theme, then init: code that registers a post type belongs on init; (4) template_redirect is the last moment to redirect before anything is drawn, and the template_include filter can swap the template the hierarchy chose (Lesson 3.1); (5) the size of the library you never edit. The line numbers are WordPress 7.1.3's and move between releases: the grep, not the number, is the skill.

Lab 1.1-B: labs/module-01/l1_1-wordpress-for-developers/wp_paths_starter.php → wp_paths_solution.php

As shipped:

WordPress root  /var/www/html/
wp-content      TODO
plugins         TODO
must-use        TODO
active theme    TODO
its URL         TODO
installed plugins: TODO
must-use plugins: TODO

The solution prints:

WordPress root  /var/www/html/
wp-content      /var/www/html/wp-content
plugins         /var/www/html/wp-content/plugins
must-use        /var/www/html/wp-content/mu-plugins
active theme    twentytwentyfive
its URL         http://localhost:8080/wp-content/themes/twentytwentyfive
installed plugins: akismet/akismet.php, hello.php
must-use plugins: mailpit-smtp.php

Accept get_template() for TODO 4 on this site (without a child theme the template and the stylesheet are the same theme; Lesson 3.4 shows where they differ) and get_template_directory_uri() for TODO 5 the same way. The point of TODO 6: some functions live in admin files that WP-CLI and the front end do not load; the Code Reference names the file.

Handout: labs/module-01/l1_1-wordpress-for-developers/debug_old_tutorial.php (deliberately old)

As shipped, WordPress prints:

Deprecated: Function get_page_by_title is <strong>deprecated</strong> since version 6.2.0! Use WP_Query instead. in /var/www/html/wp-includes/functions.php on line 6260
Sample Page ID: 2
Deprecated: Function get_userdatabylogin is <strong>deprecated</strong> since version 3.3.0! Use get_user_by('login') instead. in /var/www/html/wp-includes/functions.php on line 6260
Admin email: wordpress@example.com
Escaped: Deprecated: Function wp_specialchars is <strong>deprecated</strong> since version 2.8.0! Use esc_html() instead. in /var/www/html/wp-includes/functions.php on line 6260
Pride &amp; Prejudice &lt;new edition&gt;
[new lines in wp-content/debug.log]
[26-Oct-2026 12:01:29 UTC] PHP Deprecated:  Function get_page_by_title is <strong>deprecated</strong> since version 6.2.0! Use WP_Query instead. in /var/www/html/wp-includes/functions.php on line 6260
[26-Oct-2026 12:01:29 UTC] PHP Deprecated:  Function get_userdatabylogin is <strong>deprecated</strong> since version 3.3.0! Use get_user_by('login') instead. in /var/www/html/wp-includes/functions.php on line 6260
[26-Oct-2026 12:01:29 UTC] PHP Deprecated:  Function wp_specialchars is <strong>deprecated</strong> since version 2.8.0! Use esc_html() instead. in /var/www/html/wp-includes/functions.php on line 6260
  1. get_page_by_title(), deprecated since 6.2.0: use a query. get_posts( array( 'post_type' => 'page', 'title' => 'Sample Page', 'numberposts' => 1 ) ) returns an array; take [0].
  2. get_userdatabylogin(), since 3.3.0: get_user_by( 'login', 'admin' ).
  3. wp_specialchars(), since 2.8.0: esc_html().

Fixed (the three replacements), it prints the same three answers without a message:

Sample Page ID: 2
Admin email: wordpress@example.com
Escaped: Pride &amp; Prejudice &lt;new edition&gt;

The point: deprecated code keeps working, which is why it survives in tutorials; the messages appear only with debugging on, and each names its replacement. The oldest call here was replaced in 2009.

Lab 1.2-A (guided): labs/module-01/l1_2-local-environment/site_report_starter.sh → site_report_solution.sh

As shipped:

== Pages & Co. site report ==
WordPress: 7.1.3
Title: TODO
Admin email: TODO
Active theme: TODO
Inactive plugins: TODO
Pages: TODO

The solution prints:

== Pages & Co. site report ==
WordPress: 7.1.3
Title: Pages &amp; Co.
Admin email: wordpress@example.com
Active theme: twentytwentyfive
Inactive plugins:
akismet
hello
Pages:
+----+----------------+-------------+
| ID | post_title     | post_status |
+----+----------------+-------------+
| 2  | Sample Page    | publish     |
| 3  | Privacy Policy | draft       |
+----+----------------+-------------+

--field=name prints one value per line without a table; --fields= chooses the columns of a table. WP-CLI draws the table because the script runs in a terminal (docker compose run gives it one); piped into another command it prints tab-separated rows instead. On a learner's site that is past Session 1 the lists grow: the commands, not the rows, are the answer.

Lab 1.2-B: labs/module-01/l1_2-local-environment/store_hours_starter/ → store_hours_solution/ with test_store_hours.sh

The test with the starter's pagesco-hours.php in mu-plugins/:

Home page, the plugin's paragraph:
<p class="pagesco-hours">Open Saturday: TODO</p>
debug.log, the plugin's lines (without the time stamps):
(none yet)

With the solution's:

Home page, the plugin's paragraph:
<p class="pagesco-hours">Open Saturday: 10:00-16:00</p>
debug.log, the plugin's lines (without the time stamps):
      1 pagesco-hours: footer printed

Reading it: the must-use plugin runs on every request without activation (Lesson 1.1); wp_footer is the hook the lesson's debug demo used; esc_html() is the habit from the fixed demo; error_log() writes to debug.log because WP_DEBUG_LOG is on, and its line has no PHP level, so it is a note, not a warning. One page view, one log line: the test's own request. Remove the file afterwards (rm wordpress/wp-content/mu-plugins/pagesco-hours.php): the course site must stay as the lessons expect.

Handout: labs/module-01/l1_2-local-environment/debug_health_check.php (deliberately broken)

As shipped, PHP prints:

WordPress 7.1          OK
Site address           OK
Warning: Undefined array key "fail" in /var/www/html/debug_health_check.php on line 28
Pretty permalinks      
Environment is local   OK
Mail plugin present    OK
Warning: Undefined variable $pagesco_failed in /var/www/html/debug_health_check.php on line 33
 check(s) failed.
  1. Silent, line 18: get_option( 'permalink' ) reads an option that does not exist (false), so "Pretty permalinks" fails although permalinks are on. The option is permalink_structure (Lesson 1.2's wp option get permalink_structure).
  2. Warning, line 28: the words array has the keys ok and failed, the code asks for fail. It only shows because bug 1 made a check fail: fix bug 1 first and this bug hides until a check really fails. Fix the key anyway.
  3. Warning, line 33: the counter is $pagesco_failes (line 26) and the last line reads $pagesco_failed. Rename the counter; 0 === null is false, which is why the script claimed " check(s) failed." with no number.

Fixed, all five checks say OK and the last line is All checks passed. The lesson in it: a check that cannot fail, or fails for the wrong reason, proves nothing (the health check's Step 4).

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 1.1 · WordPress for Developers
  1. B In the pagesco-books plugin in wp-content/plugins/: It is a feature of the store's data, so it belongs in the store's own plugin, in wp-content/plugins/, where it survives core updates and theme changes.
  2. C Ask which plan they are on, because only some plans allow your own plugins: Whether you can install your own plugins on WordPress.com depends on the plan. Find out first; the answer may be an upgrade or a move to another host.
  3. A index.php, wp-blog-header.php, wp-config.php, wp-settings.php, template-loader.php: index.php hands over to wp-blog-header.php, whose wp-load.php finds wp-config.php, which ends by loading wp-settings.php; only then do wp() and the template loader run.
  4. C Replace the call with wp_get_current_user() and check how old the tutorial is: The message names the replacement. Switch to wp_get_current_user(), and treat the tutorial with care: it was written for a much older WordPress.
  5. B WordPress core runs the main program and calls your code through hooks: Core runs the program and calls your functions at the moments you registered them for with hooks. This inversion of control is what Lesson 2.3 builds on.
Lesson 1.2 · Your Local Development Environment
  1. C In a new wpcli container that is removed when the command ends: The alias runs docker compose run --rm wpcli wp: a one-off wpcli container that shares the WordPress files and the network, runs the command in /var/www/html and is removed afterwards.
  2. A Run the containers as your user (.env plus compose.override.yaml) after giving the existing files to your user: Running the wordpress and wpcli containers as your own user means you, Apache and WP-CLI all create files as the same user. Repair the existing files with a root chown in a container first.
  3. B Both are still there: the database is in a volume and the files are in wordpress/: The posts are in the db_data volume and the theme files in the wordpress folder on your disk; down only removes containers. Only down -v deletes the volume.
  4. B mailpit-smtp.php is not in wp-content/mu-plugins/, so the sender wordpress@localhost is rejected: Without mailpit-smtp.php in mu-plugins, WordPress sends from wordpress@localhost, PHPMailer rejects that address, and wp_mail returns false. Copy the plugin back.
  5. C npx @wordpress/env start, with phpVersion 8.5 in .wp-env.json: The official package is @wordpress/env, run with npx and a .wp-env.json that sets phpVersion 8.5. The unscoped wp-env package is someone else's code.

Opening: Reading the Room

There is no retrieval warm-up in Session 1. The opening's two checks have their answers above: the environment check output (what READY looks like) and the diagnostic D1 to D6. Learners who end the clinic still NOT READY leave with a numbered plan in their workbook's Start Here box.

Exit Ticket

  1. Your code: in wp-content/: your own theme, your own plugin, or a must-use plugin. Core (wp-admin/, wp-includes/, the root wp-*.php) is replaced by every update, so a change there disappears, and wp core verify-checksums (and every security scanner) reports it.
  2. Four services: wordpress (Apache and PHP 8.5 with WordPress, the site on port 8080), db (MySQL 9.7, the data in the db_data volume), wpcli (WP-CLI, started for each command and removed), mailpit (catches every email, inbox on 8025).
  3. A critical error: the end of wordpress/wp-content/debug.log: tail -n 20 wordpress/wp-content/debug.log from the project folder (or tail -f while reloading the page; Lesson 1.2's Exercise 3 makes it wplog).

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 B ("the theme folder"). Mixes up WordPress updates and theme updates. Fine to discuss: Twenty Twenty-Five IS replaced, by its own updates; pagesco is not, by anything.
  • Q2 with an en dash. Expects every title to have the separator. The home page lists posts and is titled with the site name alone; ask them to curl /hello-world/ and compare.
  • Q3 B ("wp() first"). Thinks WordPress works out the request before loading plugins. Redraw the boxes: the plugins must be loaded first, or nobody could register a post type before the query runs.
  • Q5 D ("a file per URL"). The PHP Foundations model. Show ls of the root again: there is no about.php.
  • Q9 A ("/var/www/html on my disk"). Has not connected the bind mount to the folder they edit. Open wordpress/ in VS Code next to an error message.
  • Q11 B or C. Guessing. Run wp cron test and explain what it requested.
  • Q12 with development. Remembers the four environment types but not this site's. The value comes from compose.yaml: open it.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 2: PHP the WordPress Way and the Content Model

Answers and reasoning for every Session 2 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-run items, the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 2 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up · Session 1 Revisited

  1. W1 Which folder of the WordPress root is yours, and why is nothing else? Answer: wp-content/ (themes, plugins, mu-plugins, uploads), plus your settings in wp-config.php. Everything else is core and every update replaces it.
  2. W2 Put them in order: the main query, init, the template. Answer: init (the end of loading: core, mu-plugins, plugins, the theme), then wp() runs the main query, then the template loader draws the page: load, query, template.
  3. W3 docker compose down and docker compose down -v: what survives each? Answer: down keeps the files and the database (the volume); -v deletes the database volume too.
  4. W4 A page says "There has been a critical error on this website." Where do you look first? Answer: The end of wordpress/wp-content/debug.log (tail -n 20 wordpress/wp-content/debug.log, or tail -f while reloading).
  5. W5 What does wp option get blogname print on the course site, and why? Answer: Pages &amp; Co.: WordPress stored the title HTML-escaped when it was saved. Today's first lesson explains when to escape yourself.

The site check prints, on a site exactly as Session 1 left it:

7.1.3
+--------------+----------+
| name         | status   |
+--------------+----------+
| mailpit-smtp | must-use |
+--------------+----------+

1.3 · PHP the WordPress Way: self-check

Q1
A template prints a book cover: <img src="..." alt="...">. The alt text is the book's title as staff typed it. Which function goes around the title?
A) esc_html()
B) esc_attr()
C) esc_url()
D) wp_kses_post()
Why: The value goes inside an attribute, so esc_attr() (and the attribute is quoted). Lesson 1.3's rule: escape late, for the exact place the value is printed into.
A is wrong: esc_html() is for text between tags. Here it would happen to encode the same characters, but a reviewer or PHPCS reads the context from the function: use the one that names it.
C is wrong: esc_url() is for the src and every href: it checks the protocol, which an alt text does not have.
D is wrong: wp_kses_post() keeps HTML tags a post may contain; tags inside an attribute value break the page.
Q2
From the project folder, on the course site: what does this print?
wp eval 'echo esc_html( esc_html( "Tom & Jerry" ) ), "\n";'
Tom &amp; Jerry
Why: The first esc_html() turns the ampersand into &amp;, and the second leaves an entity that is already there alone: WordPress's escapers do not double-encode (Lesson 1.3, the escape.php demo), so escaping at output is safe even when something upstream already escaped.
Q3
What reaches the page from $h = '<h2>' . _e( 'Staff picks', 'pagesco' ) . '</h2>'; echo $h;?
A) <h2>Staff picks</h2>
B) Nothing: PHP stops with a fatal error
C) "Staff picks" first, then an empty <h2></h2>
D) <h2>_e(Staff picks)</h2>
Why: _e() ECHOES the translation at once and returns null, so the text is printed while the string is still being built, and the heading ends up empty. Inside an expression you want __(), or better esc_html__() (Lesson 1.3, Common Mistakes 3). No message warns you.
A is wrong: That is what __() would give.
B is wrong: Concatenating null is allowed; nothing complains.
D is wrong: _e is a function call, not text.
Q4
Which of these needs NO pagesco_ prefix?
A) A class inside the namespace PagesCo\Books, such as PagesCo\Books\Plugin
B) A global function that formats prices
C) A variable defined at the top level of a plugin file
D) An option your plugin saves in wp_options
Why: The namespace is the prefix (Module 5's plugin uses one). Everything global shares one PHP process with core and every plugin, and a database table with them: functions, global variables, constants, hook names, options and meta keys all get the prefix.
B is wrong: Two plugins with format_price() stop the site with a fatal error.
C is wrong: A plugin file runs in the global scope, so its variables are globals.
D is wrong: Options share wp_options with core and every plugin: pagesco_books_settings, not settings.
Q5
What does this print?
wp eval 'var_dump( absint( "-7 copies" ), sanitize_key( "Staff Picks!" ) );'
int(7)
string(10) "staffpicks"
Why: absint() makes a non-negative integer: the text is cut at the first non-digit and the minus sign is dropped (validate when a negative must be REJECTED). sanitize_key() keeps lowercase letters, digits, dashes and underscores: option names and array keys.
Q6
$id = wp_insert_post( array( 'post_title' => '' ) ); What is $id, and how do you learn why it failed?
A) A WP_Error: call $id->get_error_message()
B) false: read debug.log
C) An exception: wrap the call in try/catch
D) 0: pass true as the second argument to get a WP_Error with the message
Why: By default wp_insert_post() returns 0 on failure; with true as its last argument it returns the WP_Error ("Content, title, and excerpt are empty."). Read the Return line in the Code Reference before you rely on a result (Lesson 1.3, errors.php).
A is wrong: Only when asked for with true; wp_insert_user() always returns one.
B is wrong: Nothing is logged: the failure is silent.
C is wrong: Core returns errors as values; it does not throw.

1.3 · PHP the WordPress Way: predict, then run

P1
Predict the output, then run it.
wp eval 'echo esc_url( "example.com/books?a=1&b=2" ), "\n";'
http://example.com/books?a=1&#038;b=2
Why: esc_url() adds http:// to an address without a protocol (it looks like a domain) and encodes the ampersand for HTML as &#038;. In an href the browser turns it back into &. A URL you store or redirect to uses esc_url_raw(), which leaves the & alone.
P2
Predict the output, then run it.
wp eval 'echo _n( "%s book", "%s books", 0 ), "\n";'
%s books
Why: English uses the plural for zero, so _n() picks the second string. It does NOT fill in the number: _n() only chooses the string, and sprintf() puts the count into %s (Lesson 1.3's book card does both: sprintf( _n( ... ), number_format_i18n( $n ) )).

2.1 · The Content Model: self-check

Q7
Twenty Twenty-Five's header menu lists the site's pages. Which post type stores that menu?
A) nav_menu_item
B) page
C) wp_template_part
D) wp_navigation
Why: Block themes keep their menus as wp_navigation posts (post 4 on the course site, holding a Page List block). Everything is a post with a type: one filing cabinet, many labels (Lesson 2.1).
A is wrong: nav_menu_item rows are the menus of classic themes (Module 4).
B is wrong: The pages are what the menu LISTS, not the menu.
C is wrong: The header template part contains the Navigation block; the menu itself is a separate post.
Q8
On a fresh course site (before the lesson project): what does this print, and why are the two numbers counting different things?
wp eval 'echo count( get_posts( array( "post_type" => "page" ) ) ), " ", wp_count_posts( "page" )->draft, "\n";'
1 1: one published page for get_posts() (it defaults to publish), and one draft page counted by wp_count_posts() (the Privacy Policy)
Why: get_posts() and WP_Query default to 'post_status' => 'publish', so PHP sees only the Sample Page; wp_count_posts() counts every status, and the draft Privacy Policy is one of them. WP-CLI's wp post list also lists every status unless you pass --post_status.
Q9
Devon (Author) opens Maria's published post "Book club: Dune" (ID 17). What does user_can( $devon, 'edit_post', 17 ) answer?
A) false: edit_post is a meta capability that maps to edit_others_posts here, which authors lack
B) true: authors have edit_posts
C) true: the post is published
D) An error: edit_post needs a role, not a user
Why: WordPress maps the meta capability edit_post to real capabilities from the post's author and status: someone else's published post needs edit_others_posts (and edit_published_posts). Lesson 2.1's Exercise 1 prints exactly this "no".
B is wrong: edit_posts covers his own posts only.
C is wrong: Published makes it harder, not easier: edit_published_posts joins the list.
D is wrong: user_can() takes a user (or ID); roles are never checked directly.
Q10
What does this print?
wp eval 'echo parse_blocks( "<!-- wp:separator /-->" )[0]["blockName"], "\n";'
core/separator
Why: Block markup drops the core/ namespace of core blocks (wp:separator), and parse_blocks() puts it back. A self-closing comment is a block with no saved HTML; dynamic blocks such as Latest Posts look the same and are rendered by PHP on every view.
Q11
A script creates a post with WP-CLI and then updates it twice. How many revisions does the post have, and what is in the newest one?
A) Three: the newest holds the version before the last update
B) Two: the newest is identical to the current post
C) One: WordPress keeps only the latest
D) None: only the block editor makes revisions
Why: "Every update is a revision, and the most recent revision always matches the current post" (core's own comment, shown in Lesson 2.1). Creating a post is not an update, so the first version has no revision.
A is wrong: That is the intuitive model, and it is wrong in both the count and the content.
C is wrong: WP_POST_REVISIONS is true on the course site: every revision is kept (-1).
D is wrong: WP-CLI and the editor both call wp_insert_post() / wp_update_post(); the revision comes from core.
Q12
A deployment script must set the "Site Address (URL)" field of Settings > General. Which option is that?
A) siteurl
B) blogname
C) home
D) permalink_structure
Why: Site Address is home (where visitors find the site); WordPress Address is siteurl (where WordPress's files live). They are usually equal, which is why they are easy to swap. Every Settings field is a row in wp_options (Lesson 2.1's table).
A is wrong: siteurl is the WordPress Address field.
B is wrong: blogname is the Site Title.
D is wrong: permalink_structure is Settings > Permalinks.

2.1 · The Content Model: predict, then run

P3
Predict the output, then run it.
wp post list --post_type=attachment --format=count
0
Why: Zero. Twenty Twenty-Five's pictures are files in its theme folder, not uploads: only what goes through the Media Library (or wp media import) becomes an attachment post. The lesson's demo imports one of those theme files and deletes it again.
P4
Predict the output, then run it.
wp eval 'var_dump( get_option( "page_on_front" ), get_option( "pagesco_no_such_option" ) );'
string(1) "0"
bool(false)
Why: page_on_front exists and is the STRING "0" (no static front page; options are strings). An option that does not exist returns false, which is how get_option() tells you it is missing (pass a second argument for another default).

Lab Reference Solutions

Every output below is real: the lab files run exactly as labs/check_labs.py runs them, on a fresh course site (WordPress 7.1.3, PHP 8.5, MySQL 9.7, WP-CLI 2.12), copied into the WordPress root and run with wp eval-file --use-include (PHP labs) or sh in the WP-CLI container (shell labs). Paths show /var/www/html/..., which is the learner's wordpress/ folder. All six files only read: they leave the site exactly as they found it.

Lab 1.3-A (guided): labs/module-02/l1_3-php-the-wordpress-way/signup_form_starter.php → signup_form_solution.php

As shipped:

name:  string(4) "TODO"
email: TODO
seats: TODO
shelf: TODO
field: <input name="name" value="TODO">
link:  <a href="TODO">TODO</a>

The solution prints:

name:  string(18) "Siobhan O'Neil VIP"
email: Siobhan.ONeil@Example.COM (valid: yes)
seats: 3
shelf: fiction
field: <input name="name" value="Siobhan O&#039;Neil VIP">
link:  <a href="">Siobhan O&#039;Neil VIP</a>

What each line shows: the name lost its tags but not the text between them, its line break and extra spaces, and kept its apostrophe because wp_unslash() came first; the email was trimmed (sanitize_email() keeps the case) and is_email() validated it; absint() turned -3 into 3, which a real form should REJECT instead (validate, Lesson 1.3); the shelf was not on the list, so validation fell back to fiction; esc_attr() encoded the apostrophe for the attribute; esc_url() emptied the javascript: link. Accept sanitize_text_field() without wp_unslash() only after the learner has seen O\'Neil in the output and explained it.

Lab 1.3-B: labs/module-02/l1_3-php-the-wordpress-way/event_line_starter.php → event_line_solution.php

As shipped:

TODO: Author reading: Ada Quill
TODO: Book club: Dune
TODO: Story time & cocoa
TODO: Poetry night
TODO: Book club: Dune
TODO: Poetry night

The solution prints:

skipped: The event has no date. (pagesco_no_date)
Book club: Dune on Thursday, October 1 · 1 seat left
Story time &amp; cocoa on Saturday, October 17 · 0 seats left
Author reading: Ada Quill on Thursday, November 12 · 24 seats left
Book club: Dune on Thursday, October 1
New: Author reading: Ada Quill on Nov 12 · 24 seats left

Reading it: the sort callback runs first, so the undated Poetry night (an empty string sorts first) is skipped with its WP_Error's message and code; _n() chose "1 seat left" and "0 seats left" (English uses the plural for zero); esc_html() encoded the ampersand of "Story time & cocoa"; the last two calls pass options as an array and as a query string, which wp_parse_args() accepts too. Common sticking points: the arguments of wp_parse_args() swapped (the defaults then override the caller: the debug handout's silent bug), concatenating the date into the translated string instead of using %2$s, and printing the WP_Error object ("Object of class WP_Error could not be converted to string").

Handout: labs/module-02/l1_3-php-the-wordpress-way/debug_book_badge.php (deliberately broken)

As shipped, PHP prints:

Warning: Undefined array key "autor" in /var/www/html/debug_book_badge.php on line 33
Warning: Undefined array key "price" in /var/www/html/debug_book_badge.php on line 35
Deprecated: number_format(): Passing null to parameter #1 ($num) of type float is deprecated in /var/www/html/wp-includes/functions.php on line 428
<span class="pagesco-badge">Staff pick</span> <b>The Left Hand of Darkness</b> by  $0.00
Warning: Undefined array key "autor" in /var/www/html/debug_book_badge.php on line 33
<span class="pagesco-badge">Staff pick</span> <b>Emma</b> by  $7.99
  1. Warning, line 33: $book['autor']: the key is author. Both books lose their author.
  2. Warning and Deprecated, line 35: the first book has no price, so $book['price'] is undefined (the Warning names the lab file) and the null reaches number_format() inside core (the Deprecated names wp-includes/functions.php). Read the Warning just above a core Deprecated: that is your line. Fix: print the price only when it is set (isset( $book['price'] )).
  3. Silent, line 24: the arguments of wp_parse_args() are swapped: the defaults are merged OVER the caller's options, so the second badge says "Staff pick" although the call asks for "New in paperback". wp_parse_args( $args, $defaults ): what the caller passed wins.

Fixed (the key, the isset() and the order), it prints both badges as the calls ask for, without a message:

<span class="pagesco-badge">Staff pick</span> <b>The Left Hand of Darkness</b> by Ursula K. Le Guin
<span class="pagesco-badge">New in paperback</span> <b>Emma</b> by Jane Austen $7.99

The lesson in it: messages find the typos; only reading the output against the calls finds the swapped arguments. A helper with an array of arguments needs one test call that passes every option.

Handout: labs/module-02/l2_1-content-model/debug_page_report.php (deliberately broken)

As shipped, PHP prints:

Post: Hello world!
Filed under: 
Page 2 categories:Warning: foreach() argument must be of type array|object, false given in /var/www/html/debug_page_report.php on line 24

Page 3 categories:
Pages are looked after by: Warning: Attempt to read property "display_name" on false in /var/www/html/debug_page_report.php on line 32
[new lines in wp-content/debug.log]
[26-Oct-2026 13:16:27 UTC] PHP Warning:  foreach() argument must be of type array|object, false given in /var/www/html/debug_page_report.php on line 24
[26-Oct-2026 13:16:27 UTC] PHP Warning:  Attempt to read property "display_name" on false in /var/www/html/debug_page_report.php on line 32
  1. Silent, line 19: $pagesco_post->category prints nothing and no message: WP_Post has no such field, and for an unknown property it quietly looks up post meta with that key (empty). The categories come from get_the_terms( $post, 'category' ).
  2. Warning, line 24: pages have no categories, so get_the_terms() returns false and foreach cannot loop over it. Check the result (is_array()) or ask first (is_object_in_taxonomy( 'page', 'category' )). The message shows once although the loop runs twice: PHP does not repeat the same message from the same line here.
  3. Warning, line 32: there is no user maria yet (she arrives in the lesson project), so get_user_by() returns false. Check it before you use it. After the project this bug hides: the code is still wrong.

Fixed, it prints:

Post: Hello world!
Filed under: Uncategorized
Page 2 categories: none (pages have no categories)
Page 3 categories: none (pages have no categories)
Pages are looked after by: nobody yet (no user maria)

The lesson in it: core functions say "nothing" with false or an empty value, not with an exception, and what posts and pages have is decided by their registration (Lesson 2.1's get_object_taxonomies()).

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 1.3 · PHP the WordPress Way
  1. C echo '<a href="' . esc_url( $url ) . '">Website</a>';: href and src take esc_url, which encodes the URL for HTML and drops dangerous protocols such as javascript:.
  2. A small red: wp_parse_args keeps the default size, uses the passed color, and simply carries the misspelled colour key along without using it.
  3. B if ( 0 === $count ) { pagesco_reset_count(); }: Spaces inside the parentheses, a Yoda condition with ===, and a prefixed snake_case function name.
  4. A if ( is_wp_error( $id ) ) { /* show $id->get_error_message() */ }: wp_insert_user returns the new user ID or a WP_Error object; is_wp_error tells the two apart, and the error object has the code and message.
  5. C $nick = sanitize_text_field( wp_unslash( $_POST['nickname'] ) );: Unslash first (WordPress adds slashes to request data), then sanitize on input. Escape later, when you print it.
Lesson 2.1 · The Content Model
  1. B A page: Timeless content that is linked from a menu rather than listed by date is what pages are for.
  2. C core/heading 4: Core blocks are core/name in PHP even though the comment omits the namespace, and attributes come from the JSON in the opening comment.
  3. A current_user_can( 'edit_others_posts' ): Check the capability the feature needs. Administrators, editors and any custom role that has it pass; nobody else does.
  4. B At least three; the newest is identical to the current page: Every update stores a revision of the new version, so the most recent revision always matches the current page.
  5. C In the posts_per_page option: wp option update posts_per_page 12: Every Settings field is an option; this one is posts_per_page, so wp option update posts_per_page 12 sets it.

Exit Ticket

  1. Escapers: esc_html() for text between tags, esc_attr() for an attribute value (quoted), esc_url() for every href and src; wp_kses_post() when the value is HTML a post may contain.
  2. Prefixes: core, the theme and every active plugin run in ONE PHP process with one global namespace (and share one options table): a second function with the same name is a fatal error that takes the site down, and you cannot know which plugins a client installs later. A namespace counts as a prefix for classes.
  3. The content model: all are rows of wp_posts; the post_type column tells them apart (post, page, attachment, revision, wp_navigation). That is why they share revisions, permissions and the REST API.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A (esc_html()). Thinks of escaping as one operation. It works here by accident; ask where an href goes, then show the javascript: link from Mistake 1.
  • Q2 Tom &amp;amp; Jerry. Expects double encoding. Run it: WordPress's escapers leave existing entities alone.
  • Q3 A. Has not met "_e echoes". Have them run the demo's mistake-e.php and read the third line of its output.
  • Q4 C. Thinks a variable in a plugin file is private. Plugin files run in the global scope; show $GLOBALS holding it.
  • Q6 A. Generalizes from wp_insert_user(). Read the Return line of wp_insert_post() in the Code Reference together.
  • Q7 A. Classic-theme knowledge. Run wp post get 4 --field=post_type and --field=post_content.
  • Q8 2 1 or 1 0. Expects one default for all tools. WP-CLI vs get_posts() is the lesson's statuses section.
  • Q11 A. The intuitive model of revisions. Run the lesson's revision demo again and compare the newest revision with the post.
  • Q12 A. Swaps the two addresses. WordPress Address = siteurl (files), Site Address = home (visitors).
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 3: Core Architecture, the Database and Hooks

Answers and reasoning for every Session 3 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-run items, the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 3 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up · Session 2 Revisited

  1. W1 Which escaping function goes around a URL in an href attribute? Answer: esc_url() (text between tags: esc_html(), other attribute values: esc_attr()).
  2. W2 Name the four shapes a WordPress callback can take. Which one is hard for anyone else to unhook? Answer: A function name, a closure, array( $object, 'method' ), a static method ('Class::method' or array( 'Class', 'method' )). The closure: nobody else has a handle on it (Lesson 2.3 comes back to this).
  3. W3 A page and a post: which table holds each, and which column tells them apart? Answer: Both are rows in wp_posts; post_type is page or post (Lesson 2.1's filing cabinet).
  4. W4 Code must decide whether the current user may publish pages. What does it check, and why not the role? Answer: current_user_can( 'publish_pages' ): a capability. Roles are bundles that sites change; capabilities are what WordPress checks.
  5. W5 What does wp_parse_args( $args, $defaults ) return? Answer: One array: the defaults, overridden by whatever the caller passed in $args.

The site check prints, on a site where Lesson 2.1's project was finished:

+----+-----------+----------------+-------------+
| ID | post_type | post_name      | post_author |
+----+-----------+----------------+-------------+
| 1  | post      | hello-world    | 1           |
| 2  | page      | sample-page    | 1           |
| 3  | page      | privacy-policy | 1           |
| 12 | page      | about          | 2           |
| 13 | page      | contact        | 2           |
| 14 | page      | events         | 2           |
| 15 | post      | welcome        | 2           |
| 16 | post      | staff-picks    | 3           |
| 17 | post      | book-club-dune | 2           |
+----+-----------+----------------+-------------+
+----+------------+---------------+
| ID | user_login | roles         |
+----+------------+---------------+
| 1  | admin      | administrator |
| 2  | maria      | editor        |
| 3  | devon      | author        |
+----+------------+---------------+

2.2 · Core Architecture & the Database: self-check

Q1
A tutorial says to add define( 'DISALLOW_FILE_EDIT', true ); to wp-config.php. You paste it as the very last line of the file, after require_once ABSPATH . 'wp-settings.php';. What happens?
A) It works: wp-config.php is read on every request, so the position does not matter
B) PHP stops with a parse error
C) It is defined too late: wp-settings.php has already loaded WordPress, so the file editors stay
D) WordPress moves the line above the "stop editing" comment the next time it saves the file
Why: The require_once on line 139 hands over to the loader, and WordPress reads its settings while it loads. Anything after it is defined when the decisions have been made. That is what "That's all, stop editing!" means: custom lines go between the two comments above it.
A is wrong: The position is the whole point: wp-config.php runs top to bottom, and the last line loads WordPress.
B is wrong: It is valid PHP; it simply comes too late.
D is wrong: WordPress never rewrites wp-config.php by itself.
Q2
What does this print on the course site, and what is that value?
wp config get DB_HOST
db: the name of the database service in compose.yaml (on a typical host it is localhost)
Why: wp-config.php reads it with getenv_docker('WORDPRESS_DB_HOST', ...) from the container's environment, which compose.yaml sets. Containers on one Compose network reach each other by service name. wp config get reads wp-config.php for you, so a script never needs the password.
Q3
In which order does wp-settings.php load these on every request?
A) The theme's functions.php, active plugins, must-use plugins, pluggable.php
B) Must-use plugins, active plugins, pluggable.php, the theme's functions.php
C) pluggable.php, must-use plugins, the theme's functions.php, active plugins
D) Active plugins, must-use plugins, the theme's functions.php, pluggable.php
Why: Must-use plugins at line 506, active plugins at 582, pluggable.php at 612, the theme at 741 (WordPress 7.1.3). Pluggable functions come after the plugins so a plugin can replace one by defining it first; that is also why calling one at the top of a plugin file fails.
A is wrong: That is the order backwards: the theme comes last of the four.
C is wrong: pluggable.php waits for the plugins, on purpose.
D is wrong: Must-use plugins always come before normal plugins.
Q4
Devon's first name is stored once in wp_usermeta. What does this print?
wp eval 'var_dump( get_user_meta( 3, "first_name" ) );'
array(1) {
  [0]=>
  string(5) "Devon"
}
Why: Without the third argument (true) the metadata API returns EVERY value of the key, as an array, because a key may be stored several times. With true it returns the single value, the string "Devon". Lesson 2.2, Common Mistakes 4.
Q5
Someone runs UPDATE wp_usermeta SET meta_value = REPLACE(meta_value, 'author', 'editor') on Devon's a:1:{s:6:"author";b:1;}. What happens?
A) The value breaks: unserialize() fails and Devon loses every role
B) It works, but only because both words have 6 characters: the s:6 length still matches. Change roles through WordPress anyway
C) MySQL refuses: the column holds serialized PHP
D) WordPress recomputes the lengths when it reads the value
Why: Serialized values store each string's length. Same length, still valid; "Springfield" to "Portland" was not (Lesson 2.2, Common Mistakes 1). It is still the wrong tool: it skips WordPress's caches and hooks, and the next REPLACE may not be so lucky. wp user set-role devon editor or $user->set_role() is the API.
A is wrong: That happens when the length changes; here it does not.
C is wrong: To MySQL it is just LONGTEXT.
D is wrong: WordPress trusts the stored lengths; a mismatch makes unserialize() fail.
Q6
An option's autoload column says auto-off. What does that mean?
A) The code that saved it passed false
B) It is loaded on every request
C) The option is broken and will be deleted
D) Nobody chose, and WordPress decided not to autoload it, usually because the value is large (over 150,000 bytes)
Why: on/off = the code said true/false; auto = nobody said, WordPress's default (loaded today); auto-on/auto-off = WordPress decided by itself. Lesson 2.2's autoload demo: a 200,000-byte option became auto-off. Rule for your plugins: pass true for small settings you read on every page, false for anything big or rare.
A is wrong: Passing false gives off.
B is wrong: auto-off is NOT in the list wp_autoload_values_to_autoload() returns.
C is wrong: Nothing about it is broken; it is read on demand, with one query.

2.2 · Core Architecture & the Database: predict, then run

P1
Predict the output, then run it.
wp eval 'var_dump( get_option( "pagesco_no_such_option" ), get_option( "pagesco_no_such_option", "none" ) );'
bool(false)
string(4) "none"
Why: An option that does not exist returns false, or the default you pass as the second argument. Both reads cost a query (WordPress cannot know an option is missing without asking; the lesson's options-cost.php measured it).
P2
Predict the output, then run it.
wp db query "SELECT option_value FROM wp_options WHERE option_name = 'active_plugins'"
option_value
a:0:{}
Why: a:0:{} is a serialized empty array: no active plugins. wp db query prints the column name, then the raw value; get_option( 'active_plugins' ) would return array().

2.3 · The Hooks System: self-check

Q7
What does this print when run with wp eval-file?
<?php
add_filter( 'pagesco_q7', fn( $text ) => $text . 'b', 20 );
add_filter( 'pagesco_q7', fn( $text ) => strtoupper( $text ) );
add_filter( 'pagesco_q7', fn( $text ) => $text . 'a', 5 );

echo apply_filters( 'pagesco_q7', 'x' ), "\n";
XAb
Why: Priority 5 runs first (xa), then the callback at the default 10 (XA), then 20 (XAb). The order in which the lines were written does not matter across priorities; only ties at the same priority keep their added order.
Q8
A members page must send visitors who are not logged in to the login screen. On which action do you call wp_safe_redirect() and exit?
A) plugins_loaded
B) wp_footer
C) template_redirect
D) shutdown
Why: template_redirect fires when WordPress knows which page was asked for (conditional tags work) and before any HTML is printed, so headers can still be sent. Lesson 2.3's table of actions.
A is wrong: Too early: the request has not been parsed, so you cannot know which page it is.
B is wrong: Too late: the page has been printed, headers are gone.
D is wrong: Far too late: the response is finished.
Q9
A theme adds add_action( 'wp_head', 'pagesco_theme_banner' ) inside its after_setup_theme callback. Your plugin calls remove_action( 'wp_head', 'pagesco_theme_banner' ) at the top of its main file, and the banner stays. Why?
A) The plugin ran before the theme added the hook, so there was nothing to remove: remove it on init
B) remove_action() only works on core's hooks
C) The priority is wrong: it must be 1
D) Plugins cannot change what a theme does
Why: Removal rule 2 of Lesson 2.3: remove after it was added. Plugins load at line 582 of wp-settings.php, the theme at 741 and its after_setup_theme callbacks after that; init (line 779) comes later. remove_action() returned false, silently.
B is wrong: It works on any hook, if the hook, callback and priority match and the hook exists already.
C is wrong: The theme added it at the default 10, so 10 (the default) is right.
D is wrong: They can, with hooks; that is the point of them.
Q10
What does this print, and what does the value mean?
wp eval 'var_dump( has_filter( "the_content", "do_blocks" ) );'
int(9): do_blocks is attached to the_content at priority 9, before wpautop at 10
Why: has_filter() with a callback returns its priority, or false when it is not attached. do_blocks turns block markup into HTML before the paragraph filter runs (Lesson 2.1 named it; Exercise 3 of 2.3 lists them all). Test the result with false !== has_filter( ... ): 0 is a valid priority.
Q11
After a new the_title filter, posts have no titles and the only message is Deprecated: str_replace(): Passing null to parameter #3 ... in .../wp-includes/formatting.php on line 5777. What is the likely bug?
A) Your callback does not return the title, so the next callback (core's, at priority 11) received null
B) A bug in WordPress 7.1's formatting.php: update core
C) PHP 8.5 deprecated str_replace()
D) The filter needs 2 accepted arguments
Why: A filter must return the value. Null went down the chain to capital_P_dangit(), which handed it to str_replace(), so the message points into core. When a message blames a core file, look at what YOUR code handed to it (Lesson 2.3, Common Mistakes 1).
B is wrong: Core is fine; it received a null it did not expect.
C is wrong: str_replace() is fine; passing null to it is deprecated.
D is wrong: That mistake is a fatal ArgumentCountError, not a Deprecated notice.
Q12
Your plugin prints a gift-card form. Other developers should be able to print a line of their own just BEFORE the form. What do you add right before you print it?
A) apply_filters( 'pagesco_before_gift_form', '' );
B) add_action( 'pagesco_before_gift_form', 'pagesco_print_form' );
C) do_action( 'before_gift_form' );
D) do_action( 'pagesco_before_gift_form' ); with a DocBlock above it
Why: A moment others may act at is an action; you fire it with do_action(), prefixed and documented like core's (the project's pagesco_before_store_notice). A value others may change would be a filter.
A is wrong: A filter is for a value; nobody is asked to change one here.
B is wrong: add_action() attaches a callback; it does not create the moment.
C is wrong: Unprefixed: another plugin's before_gift_form would collide with it.

2.3 · The Hooks System: predict, then run

P3
Predict the output, then run it.
wp eval 'add_filter( "pagesco_p", "strrev" ); echo apply_filters( "pagesco_p", "Dune" ), " ", apply_filters( "pagesco_other", "Dune" ), "\n";'
enuD Dune
Why: strrev is a callback by name: PHP's own function, which takes one value and returns one, the shape a filter needs. A hook nobody filters returns the value unchanged, which is why calling apply_filters() costs nothing when no plugin uses it.
P4
Predict the output, then run it.
wp eval 'add_action( "pagesco_p", function () { echo "ran "; } ); var_dump( do_action( "pagesco_p" ) );'
ran NULL
Why: The action ran its callback (ran ), and do_action() returns nothing: var_dump() of nothing is NULL. Actions are moments, not values; if you need an answer back, offer a filter.

Lab Reference Solutions

Every output below is real: the lab files run exactly as labs/check_labs.py runs them, on the course site at the end of Lesson 2.1 (WordPress 7.1.3, PHP 8.5, MySQL 9.7, WP-CLI 2.12), copied into the WordPress root and run with wp eval-file --use-include (PHP labs) or sh in the WP-CLI container (shell labs). Paths show /var/www/html/..., which is the learner's wordpress/ folder.

Lab 2.2-A (guided): labs/module-03/l2_2-core-database/meta_pivot_starter.sh → meta_pivot_solution.sh

As shipped:

== Step 1: the users ==
ID	user_login	display_name
1	admin	admin
2	maria	Maria Lopez
3	devon	Devon Park

== Step 2: first names, one join ==
Step 2: TODO
== Step 3: one row per user, two fields ==
Step 3: TODO
== Step 4: the same through WordPress ==
Step 4: TODO
== Step 5: meta rows per user ==
Step 5: TODO

The solution prints:

== Step 1: the users ==
ID	user_login	display_name
1	admin	admin
2	maria	Maria Lopez
3	devon	Devon Park

== Step 2: first names, one join ==
ID	user_login	first_name
1	admin	
2	maria	Maria
3	devon	Devon

== Step 3: one row per user, two fields ==
ID	user_login	first_name	last_name
1	admin		
2	maria	Maria	Lopez
3	devon	Devon	Park

== Step 4: the same through WordPress ==
+----+------------+------------+-----------+
| ID | user_login | first_name | last_name |
+----+------------+------------+-----------+
| 1  | admin      |            |           |
| 2  | maria      | Maria      | Lopez     |
| 3  | devon      | Devon      | Park      |
+----+------------+------------+-----------+
== Step 5: meta rows per user ==
user_id	meta_rows
1	16
2	15
3	16

The key query is Step 3: one join without a key condition gives every meta row of every user; GROUP BY folds them into one row per user, and each MAX(CASE WHEN um.meta_key = 'x' THEN um.meta_value END) keeps the one non-NULL value of its key (MAX of one value is that value). Common wrong turns: the key condition in WHERE (then only one key survives the join and the other column is NULL); a second join per field (also correct: one join per field, the shape WP_Query writes for two meta conditions). admin's names are empty strings, not NULL: the rows exist. Step 5's counts grow when a learner changes screen options or opens the editor (WordPress stores per-user preferences as meta).
