TODO 5 is the list from the lesson's version note: what wp_load_alloptions() loads; auto-off is not in it.

Handout: labs/module-03/l2_2-core-database/debug_meta_report.php (deliberately broken)

As shipped, PHP prints:

Post 15 waiting for pings: no
Warning: foreach() argument must be of type array|object, string given in /var/www/html/debug_meta_report.php on line 26
Published pages: Warning: Undefined property: stdClass::$published in /var/www/html/debug_meta_report.php on line 32
[new lines in wp-content/debug.log]
[26-Oct-2026 14:09:36 UTC] PHP Warning:  foreach() argument must be of type array|object, string given in /var/www/html/debug_meta_report.php on line 26
[26-Oct-2026 14:09:36 UTC] PHP Warning:  Undefined property: stdClass::$published in /var/www/html/debug_meta_report.php on line 32
  1. Silent, line 21: get_post_meta( 15, '_pingme' ) without true returns an array (array( '1' )), never identical to the string '1', so the answer is "no" although the row exists. Pass true (Lesson 2.2, Common Mistakes 4).
  2. Warning, line 26 (the bug is on line 25): the meta key is wp_capabilities, with the table prefix. get_user_meta() of a key that does not exist returns '' with true, and foreach() on a string warns. In code the key is $wpdb->prefix . 'capabilities' (the prefix can be anything); better still, get_userdata( 2 )->roles.
  3. Warning, line 32: wp_count_posts() names its properties after the statuses: publish, not published.

Fixed, it prints:

Post 15 waiting for pings: yes
Maria's role: editor
Published pages: 4

Two of the three bugs are the lesson's own lessons in disguise (the third argument, the prefix); the third is the most ordinary bug in PHP: a guessed property name. wp db query "SELECT meta_key FROM wp_usermeta WHERE user_id = 2" answers the second one in one line.

Lab 2.3-A (guided): labs/module-03/l2_3-hooks/filter_chain_starter.php → filter_chain_solution.php

As shipped:

Removed the sale badge: TODO
pagesco_lab_capitalize: TODO
Line: fahrenheit 451 [SALE]

The solution prints:

Removed the sale badge: true
pagesco_lab_capitalize: 5
pagesco_lab_add_author: 10
pagesco_lab_sale_badge: false
Line: Fahrenheit 451 by Ray Bradbury

What each TODO shows: (1) priority 5 runs before the default 10, so the title is capitalized before the author is added; (2) 10, 2 hands the callback the hook's second value, the author (without it: the fatal ArgumentCountError of Lesson 2.3, Mistake 2); (3) the badge was added at 30, so only remove_filter( ..., 30 ) removes it, and the true says so; (4) has_filter() returns the priority, and false for the removed badge. Accept ucwords written inside the author callback only if the learner can say why the separate callback is better (another plugin can remove or reorder it).

Lab 2.3-B: labs/module-03/l2_3-hooks/hook_finder_starter.sh → hook_finder_solution.sh

As shipped:

== 1. Where core fires wp_head ==
wp-includes/general-template.php:3441:	do_action( 'wp_head' );
== 2. Who adds the generator tag ==
2. TODO
== 3. Where the excerpt length is filtered ==
3. TODO
== 4. Asked of the running site ==
4. TODO
== 5. Callbacks on wp_head ==
5. TODO

The solution prints:

== 1. Where core fires wp_head ==
wp-includes/general-template.php:3441:	do_action( 'wp_head' );
== 2. Who adds the generator tag ==
362:add_action( 'wp_head', 'wp_generator' );
== 3. Where the excerpt length is filtered ==
wp-includes/formatting.php:4081:		$excerpt_length = (int) apply_filters( 'excerpt_length', $excerpt_length );
wp-includes/blocks/latest-posts.php:167:				$excerpt_length = (int) apply_filters( 'excerpt_length', $block_core_latest_posts_excerpt_length );
== 4. Asked of the running site ==
wpautop on the_content: 10
== 5. Callbacks on wp_head ==
30

Patterns must be exact: grep -rn wp_head wp-includes/ returns every line that mentions it (callbacks attached to it, comments, the function wp_head() itself). With the quotes and spaces core's coding standard uses, do_action( 'wp_head' ) is found once, at line 3441 of general-template.php, and the generator at line 362 of default-filters.php. Step 3 finds two callers: wp_trim_excerpt() in formatting.php (the automatic excerpt) and the Latest Posts block, which is why one filter changes both. Step 5's 30 counts the callbacks on wp_head of a WP-CLI run on this site; a front-end request can differ, because themes and plugins add their own. Line numbers move between releases; the pattern is the skill.

Lab 2.3-C: labs/module-03/l2_3-hooks/title_parts_starter/ → title_parts_solution/ with test_title_parts.sh

The test with the starter's pagesco-title.php in mu-plugins/:

/                 <title>Pages &amp; Co. &#8211; TODO</title>
/book-club-dune/  <title>Book club: Dune &#8211; Pages &amp; Co.</title>
/hello-world/     <title>Hello world! &#8211; Pages &amp; Co.</title>
/about/           <title>About Us &#8211; Pages &amp; Co.</title>

With the solution's:

/                 <title>Pages &amp; Co. &#8211; Independent bookstore since 1998</title>
/book-club-dune/  <title>News: Book club: Dune &#8211; Pages &amp; Co.</title>
/hello-world/     <title>Hello world! &#8211; Pages &amp; Co.</title>
/about/           <title>About Us &#8211; Pages &amp; Co.</title>

Reading it: document_title_parts passes an array (title, page, tagline, site) and the callback returns the array, changed or not; WordPress then joins the parts with the separator and escapes them, so & arrives as &amp;. The front page is the blog home here (Settings > Reading: your latest posts), so its parts are the site name and the tagline. in_category( 'news' ) takes the slug and only Book club: Dune of the four tested pages is a News post; Hello world! (Uncategorized) and About Us stay as they were. The TODO 3 trap: a return only inside the if, which empties every other title. Remove the file afterwards (rm wordpress/wp-content/mu-plugins/pagesco-title.php).

Handout: labs/module-03/l2_3-hooks/debug_price_tag.php (deliberately broken)

As shipped, PHP prints:

Deprecated: number_format(): Passing null to parameter #1 ($num) of type float is deprecated in /var/www/html/debug_price_tag.php on line 26
Warning: Undefined variable $pagesco_shop_name in /var/www/html/debug_price_tag.php on line 36
 price: $0.00 (holiday price)
  1. Deprecated, line 26 (the bug is on line 21): pagesco_lab_round_price() assigns the rounded price and returns nothing, so the next callback receives null and number_format() complains. The message points at an innocent line, the pattern of Lesson 2.3's Mistake 1. Fix: return round( $price, 2 );.
  2. Warning, line 36: a function cannot see variables from outside it, so $pagesco_shop_name is undefined inside the callback and the label starts with nothing. Fix: the name inside the function (or a constant). global $pagesco_shop_name; does NOT help here: a file run by wp eval-file is included inside a WP-CLI method, so its top-level variables are local to that method, not globals. Measured with a three-line probe, global gives:
  3. Silent, line 41: the markup was added at priority 15, so remove_filter() without the 15 looks at 10, finds nothing and returns false; "(holiday price)" stays. Fix: pass 15, and var_dump() the result while you debug.
NULL

Fixed (all three, with the removal's result printed), it prints:

bool(true)
Pages & Co. price: $14.99

Fixing bug 1 first changes the Deprecated message into a correct price, which makes the silent bug easy to spot: the markup is visible on a real number. That order is worth asking learners to explain.

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 2.2 · Core Architecture & the Database
  1. A A row in wp_postmeta with the post's ID, a meta_key and a meta_value: Extra fields of a post are rows in wp_postmeta: post_id, meta_key, meta_value. That is what update_post_meta writes.
  2. B 10, 100, 9: meta_value is LONGTEXT, so the values are compared as text, character by character: 1 sorts before 9. Cast them, or use meta_value_num in WP_Query, for numeric order.
  3. C auto: With no decision from the code and a small value, WordPress stores auto, which is autoloaded today. Large values become auto-off.
  4. B It stays in wp_postmeta as orphaned rows: WordPress tables have no foreign keys, so MySQL neither refuses nor cascades. The meta and term rows stay as orphans; wp_delete_post would have removed them.
  5. C pluggable.php, which defines it, loads only after all plugins: wp_get_current_user lives in pluggable.php, which wp-settings.php loads after all plugins so they can replace its functions. Call it from a hook such as init instead.
Lesson 2.3 · The Hooks System
  1. B add_filter( 'the_title', ... ) with a callback that returns the title and an asterisk: the_title is a filter: WordPress passes the title in and displays whatever your callback returns, so the callback returns the title with an asterisk added.
  2. C CBDA: C has priority 5 and runs first. B and D both have the default 10 and run in the order they were added, then A at 20.
  3. A remove_filter( 'excerpt_length', 'pagesco_excerpt_length', 15 );: Removal needs the same hook, the same callback and the same priority it was added with, here 15.
  4. C ArgumentCountError: Too few arguments to function {closure}(), 1 passed ... and exactly 2 expected: add_filter defaults to one accepted argument, so the closure is called with only the title and PHP throws ArgumentCountError for the missing $id. Registering with 10, 2 fixes it.
  5. A $count = apply_filters( 'pagesco_featured_count', 3 );: A prefixed, documented filter on the value is exactly the extension point: the other developer hooks pagesco_featured_count and returns a different number.

Exit Ticket

  1. Through WordPress: WordPress's tables have no foreign keys, so PHP does what ON DELETE CASCADE would do (wp_delete_post() removes meta, term links, revisions and comments); serialized values carry their lengths, which a text REPLACE breaks; and the API keeps the object cache and every hook in step. A DELETE at the prompt leaves orphans; an UPDATE skips the caches.
  2. Banner and excerpts: a banner at the top of every page is a moment: an action on wp_body_open (the project's store notice). The excerpt length is a value: a filter on excerpt_length whose callback returns a number.
  3. remove_action() false: the priority differs from the one used in add_action(); it ran before the hook was added (a theme's after_setup_theme callback, removed from the top of a plugin file); or the callback is not the same one (a closure, or a method of a different object).

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A ("position does not matter"). Sees wp-config.php as a list of settings, not a program that ends by loading WordPress. Show line 139 again.
  • Q3 A or D. Remembers that the theme is "the outside" and plugins "the inside" but not the order. Redraw the shop: lights, always-there things, today's departments, window display.
  • Q4 with string(5) "Devon". Expects one value because there is one row. The metadata API always returns a list unless you ask for one value: true.
  • Q5 A. Correct instinct (never edit serialized text), wrong mechanism: count the letters. The answer's second half (use the API anyway) is the part to keep.
  • Q7 with XbA or xaB. Ran the lines in the order written. Ask: "which priority runs first?"
  • Q9 C. Blames the priority, the most common removal bug, but this one is timing. Ask: "when did the theme add it, and when did your line run?"
  • Q11 D. Mixes up the two the_title mistakes: a missing return is a Deprecated notice and an empty title; two arguments asked and one registered is a fatal error.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 4: Theme Anatomy and Building a Block Theme

Answers and reasoning for every Session 4 item: the warm-up, Checkpoint 1's model answer and rubric, both self-checks, the predict-then-run items, the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 4 12 self-check items Checkpoint 1 Confidential

Warm-up

Real output on the site of Lesson 2.3.

W1
Session 3: priorities: What does this print on the site of Lesson 2.3?
wp eval 'echo has_filter( "pagesco_store_notice", "pagesco_notice_late_opening" ), "\n";'
20
Why: The priority: has_filter() returns the priority of that callback on that hook, or false. pagesco-site.php adds the late-opening text at 20, so it runs after a callback at the default 10.
W2
Session 3: must-use plugins: Which two names does this list on your site, and why does neither need activating?
wp plugin list --status=must-use --fields=name,status
+--------------+----------+
| name         | status   |
+--------------+----------+
| mailpit-smtp | must-use |
| pagesco-site | must-use |
+--------------+----------+
Why: mailpit-smtp (Lesson 1.2) and pagesco-site (Lesson 2.3). Every PHP file in wp-content/mu-plugins/ runs on every request, before the normal plugins (the load order of Lesson 2.2).
W3
Session 3: removing a hook: Why does this print 0?
curl -s http://localhost:8080/about/ | grep -c 'name="generator"'
0
Why: pagesco-site.php calls remove_action( 'wp_head', 'wp_generator' ), so the generator meta tag is never printed (and grep -c with no match exits 1).

Checkpoint 1 · Model Answer

The task is on the workbook's Checkpoint 1 page; the starter is labs/module-04/checkpoint-1/checkpoint_starter/pagesco-checkpoint.php. The model answer, labs/module-04/checkpoint-1/checkpoint_solution/pagesco-checkpoint.php:

<?php
/**
 * Plugin Name: Pages & Co. Checkpoint 1
 * Description: Checkpoint 1 (Modules 1 and 2), model answer: three small additions to the store notice and the footer.
 */

/**
 * (a) Adds the newest News post to the store notice.
 *
 * @param string $text The notice text so far (plain text).
 * @return string
 */
function pagesco_checkpoint_latest_news( $text ) {
	$posts = get_posts(
		array(
			'category_name' => 'news',
			'numberposts'   => 1,
		)
	);

	if ( ! $posts ) {
		return $text;
	}

	return $text . ' Latest news: ' . get_the_title( $posts[0] ) . '.';
}
add_filter( 'pagesco_store_notice', 'pagesco_checkpoint_latest_news', 30 );

/**
 * (b) Prints a link to Maria's author archive inside the notice bar.
 */
function pagesco_checkpoint_ask_link() {
	$user = get_user_by( 'login', 'maria' );

	if ( ! $user ) {
		return;
	}

	echo ' · <a href="' . esc_url( get_author_posts_url( $user->ID ) ) . '">' . esc_html( 'Ask ' . $user->display_name ) . '</a>';
}
add_action( 'pagesco_store_notice_end', 'pagesco_checkpoint_ask_link', 20 );

/**
 * (c) Prints the number of published pages in the footer.
 */
function pagesco_checkpoint_page_count() {
	global $wpdb;

	$count = (int) $wpdb->get_var(
		$wpdb->prepare(
			"SELECT COUNT(*) FROM {$wpdb->posts} WHERE post_type = %s AND post_status = %s",
			'page',
			'publish'
		)
	);

	echo '<p class="pagesco-pages">Published pages: ' . esc_html( $count ) . '</p>';
}
add_action( 'wp_footer', 'pagesco_checkpoint_page_count' );

test_checkpoint.sh with the model answer in mu-plugins/:

notice bar:
<div class="pagesco-notice">Free shipping on orders over $35. Open until 9 p.m. on Thursdays. Latest news: Book club: Dune. <a href="http://localhost:8080/events/">See events</a> · <a href="http://localhost:8080/author/maria/">Ask Maria Lopez</a></div>
footer line:
<p class="pagesco-pages">Published pages: 4</p>

With the starter as handed out (only the two add_action lines are there, so (a) does not run yet):

notice bar:
<div class="pagesco-notice">Free shipping on orders over $35. Open until 9 p.m. on Thursdays. <a href="http://localhost:8080/events/">See events</a> · TODO</div>
footer line:
<p class="pagesco-pages">Published pages: TODO</p>
Rubric (2 / 1 / 0 per row, 10 points)
Criterion2 · Meets1 · Partly0 · Not yet
Hooks, names and prioritiesadd_filter( 'pagesco_store_notice', ..., 30 ) and add_action( 'pagesco_store_notice_end', ..., 20 ) with the callbacks named as strings; the filter RETURNS the text; the order in the bar is late opening, latest news, events link, Ask Maria.Works, but a priority is off (Ask Maria before See events) or the filter is attached with add_action.The filter echoes instead of returning (the notice text disappears), or a hook name is misspelled.
WordPress functions with argumentsget_posts() with an arguments array (category_name news, numberposts 1); get_user_by( 'login', 'maria' ); get_author_posts_url(); each result checked before use.Right answer by a detour (all posts fetched and filtered in PHP, the URL typed by hand, the title or name hard-coded).The newest post or Maria's link is wrong or missing.
Escaping at outputesc_url() on the link and esc_html() on its text; the filter returns PLAIN text, because the notice escapes it (Lesson 2.3).One part unescaped, or the filter's text escaped a second time.User data printed with no escaping at all.
A prepared queryONE $wpdb->get_var( $wpdb->prepare( ... ) ) on {$wpdb->posts} with %s for page and publish; the count printed as a number.Correct count, but the values are written into the SQL string, or the table name is typed as wp_posts.No query (a number typed in, or the count of the wrong post type).
Clean, standard codeThe test prints the three expected lines and NO message in the page or in debug.log; every function has the pagesco_ prefix and a DocBlock; WordPress spacing and tabs.One message, or the prefix or DocBlocks missing on some functions.The site shows a warning or a critical error, or the work is unfinished.
  • Equally good: get_posts() with 'category' and the term ID 2, or 'posts_per_page' => 1; WP_Query with 'no_found_rows' => true; wp_count_posts( 'page' )->publish for (c) is correct WordPress but misses the point of the task (rubric row 4: 1 point, "no prepared query"). Any order of escaping inside the link is fine as long as both parts are escaped once.
  • Common wrong answers: the filter echoes (the notice loses its text; row 1: 0); priority 10 on (b) ("Ask Maria" before "See events", because pagesco-checkpoint.php loads before pagesco-site.php: mu-plugins load alphabetically); esc_html() on the returned notice text (&amp; would show twice if a title had one; row 3: 1); "... WHERE post_type = 'page'" without prepare() (row 4: 1); a hard-coded "Book club: Dune" (row 2: 0, and it breaks with the next News post).

3.1 · Theme Anatomy & the Template Hierarchy: self-check

Q1
Twenty Twenty-Five is active. Which candidate list does WordPress build for the About page, /about/ (page ID 12)?
A) about, page, index
B) page-about, page-12, page, singular, index
C) single-page-about, single-page, single, singular, index
D) page-12, page-about, page, index
Why: Pages go slug first, then ID, then page, singular and index; Lesson 3.1's template-map log shows exactly page-about.php, page-12.php, page.php and then the fall-through to singular and index.
A is wrong: Hierarchy names are type-slug, never the slug alone.
C is wrong: single-{type}-{slug} is the chain of posts and custom post types; pages have their own.
D is wrong: The slug comes before the ID, and singular is missing.
Q2
What does this print on the course site?
wp eval 'echo implode( ", ", get_template_hierarchy( "author-devon" ) ), "\n";'
author-devon, author, archive, index
Why: Devon is user 3, but get_template_hierarchy() works from the slug alone, so the ID-based name author-3 that a real request adds is missing (Lesson 3.1, Exercise 1). Every archive passes through archive; every list ends in index.
Q3
A client's classic theme has wp_head() in header.php and wp_footer() in footer.php, but no wp_body_open(). What happens to the Pages & Co. store notice?
A) A PHP warning names the missing function
B) WordPress calls wp_body_open itself, as it does for block themes
C) The notice moves to the footer
D) It disappears, with no message anywhere
Why: pagesco-site.php adds its callback to wp_body_open, and in a classic theme only the theme fires that action. Nobody fires it, so nothing is printed and nothing is wrong as far as PHP knows (Lesson 3.1, Common Mistakes 3).
A is wrong: A hook that never fires is not an error.
B is wrong: Only template-canvas.php, for block themes, fires it on the theme's behalf.
C is wrong: A callback runs only on the hook it was added to.
Q4
Every function in Twenty Twenty-Five's functions.php is wrapped in if ( ! function_exists( ... ) ). Why?
A) So a child theme can define a function of the same name first and replace it
B) So WordPress can load functions.php twice
C) PHP 8.5 requires it for theme functions
D) So the functions run only in the block editor
Why: A child theme's functions.php is loaded BEFORE its parent's. If it defines twentytwentyfive_editor_style(), the parent skips its own: that is how a theme someone else maintains is customized without editing it (Lesson 3.4).
B is wrong: Loading it twice would still be a mistake; the guard is about replacing, not repeating.
C is wrong: PHP has no such rule; redeclaring a function is simply a fatal error.
D is wrong: function_exists() says nothing about where code runs.
Q5
Settings > Reading shows the latest posts on the front page. The theme has front-page.html, home.html and index.html. Which template draws http://localhost:8080/?
A) home.html
B) index.html
C) front-page.html
D) The theme cannot have both front-page.html and home.html
Why: The front page's list is front-page, home, index: front-page comes first whatever the front page shows. home is only the list of latest posts, used here when the theme has no front-page.
A is wrong: home would win only without a front-page template.
B is wrong: index is the last resort.
D is wrong: Both are normal: front-page for the front page, home for the blog index wherever it lives.
Q6
Name the filter that changes the list of candidate templates for category archives, before WordPress looks for files.
category_template_hierarchy
Why: Each type has its own {$type}_template_hierarchy filter (template.php line 62); Lesson 3.1's template-map tool hooks all eighteen. template_include is the other hook: it filters the final file path, after the choice.

3.1 · Theme Anatomy & the Template Hierarchy: predict, then run

P1
Predict the output, then run it.
wp eval 'echo implode( ", ", get_template_hierarchy( "front-page" ) ), "\n";'
front-page, home, index
Why: front-page first whatever the front page shows, then home (the latest posts), then index. With a static front page the chain after front-page is the page chain instead (Lesson 3.1, Mistake 2).
P2
Predict the output, then run it.
wp eval 'echo count( get_block_templates( array(), "wp_template_part" ) ), "\n";'
7
Why: Twenty Twenty-Five has seven parts: header, vertical-header, header-large-title, footer, footer-columns, footer-newsletter and sidebar. get_block_templates( array(), 'wp_template_part' ) lists parts instead of templates.

3.2 · Building a Block Theme: self-check

Q7
On the course site after Lesson 3.2 (pagesco active), what does this print?
wp eval 'echo wp_get_global_settings( array( "layout", "wideSize" ) ), "\n";'
1180px
Why: wp_get_global_settings() reads the merged theme.json data (core, theme, Site Editor) by path: settings.layout.wideSize is pagesco's 1180px. Code that needs a design value asks for it instead of repeating the number.
Q8
In theme.json, "fontSize": "var:preset|font-size|large" under styles. What does WordPress print in the page's CSS?
A) font-size: large
B) font-size: 1.75rem
C) font-size: var(--wp--preset--font-size--large)
D) font-size: var(--large)
Why: The shorthand points at the preset, and the preset is a CSS custom property, so a style variation or the Styles panel can change the size in one place. Lesson 3.2's body rule shows the same for colors: background-color: var(--wp--preset--color--paper).
A is wrong: That would be the CSS keyword large, not your preset.
B is wrong: The value is never copied in: that is the point of presets.
D is wrong: Custom properties from theme.json always carry the --wp--preset--TYPE-- prefix (here --wp--preset--font-size--).
Q9
Which of these blocks is written SELF-CLOSING in a template file, with no HTML of its own?
A) Group
B) Heading
C) Paragraph
D) Post Title
Why: Post Title is dynamic: PHP renders it on every request from the post being viewed, so the file holds only <!-- wp:post-title {"level":1} /-->. Group, Heading and Paragraph are static: their exact saved HTML sits between the comments.
A is wrong: Group is static: its <div> (or <main>) is in the file.
B is wrong: Heading is static, with its <h1> in the file.
C is wrong: Paragraph is static, with its <p> in the file.
Q10
A live site (WP_DEBUG off) gets a theme.json with a trailing comma. What do visitors see?
A) The site in core's default design: the theme's colors, fonts and sizes are gone, with no message
B) A critical error page
C) The notice wp_json_file_decode(): Syntax error at the top of every page
D) Nothing changes: WordPress skips the bad line
Why: WordPress cannot parse the file, so it uses no theme.json at all (zero oxblood variables in Lesson 3.2's Mistake 1). The Notice exists, but only with WP_DEBUG on, in the page and in debug.log. The notice names the file, not the line: $schema in VS Code finds it.
B is wrong: It is a Notice, not a fatal error: the page still renders.
C is wrong: Only with WP_DEBUG and WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY on, as on the course site.
D is wrong: JSON is parsed as a whole; one syntax error loses the whole file.
Q11
A template contains <!-- wp:template-part {"slug":"footer","tagName":"footer"} /-->. Which file of the theme does it include, and what does the tagName do?
parts/footer.html; it wraps the part in a <footer> element (the contentinfo landmark)
Why: The slug is the file name in parts/ without .html (a typo such as heder renders nothing for visitors). tagName gives screen-reader users the header, main and footer landmarks; Lesson 3.2 checks them with curl.
Q12
What does "defaultPalette": false in settings.color do?
A) Deletes the theme's own palette
B) Hides WordPress's own palette from the editors, so they choose from the theme's colors
C) Turns off all color controls
D) Removes --wp--preset--color--black and --white from the page
Why: The editors then offer only Paper, Ink, Oxblood, Sage and Slate. Core still prints black and white as custom properties (Lesson 3.2's preset grep shows both). Turning off the free color picker is a different setting, custom: false (the lesson's quiz Q4).
A is wrong: Your palette is what remains.
C is wrong: Controls stay; only the default swatches go.
D is wrong: Both are still printed: the lesson's output starts with them.

3.2 · Building a Block Theme: predict, then run

P3
Predict the output, then run it.
curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n' http://localhost:8080/nothing-here/
404
Why: pagesco's 404.html draws the page, and WordPress still answers with status 404 so search engines do not index it. Lesson 3.2's verification loop checks exactly this.
P4
Predict the output, then run it.
curl -s http://localhost:8080/ | grep -o '\.has-oxblood-color{[^}]*}'
.has-oxblood-color{color: var(--wp--preset--color--oxblood) !important;}
Why: Every palette color produces a class rule using its custom property, with !important so a color chosen for one block beats the theme's other rules.

Lab Reference Solutions

Every output below is real: the lab files run exactly as labs/check_labs.py runs them, on the course site at the state each file names (WordPress 7.1.3, PHP 8.5, MySQL 9.7, WP-CLI 2.12): the 3.1 labs on the site of Lesson 2.3, the 3.2 labs on the site of Lesson 3.1. Paths show /var/www/html/..., which is the learner's wordpress/ folder.

Lab 3.1-A (guided): labs/module-04/l3_1-theme-anatomy/which_template_starter.php → which_template_solution.php

As shipped:

/tag/staff-picks/   tag-staff-picks > tag > archive > index
                    => TODO
/author/devon/      author-devon > author > archive > index
                    => TODO
/hello-world/       single-post-hello-world > single-post > single > singular > index

The solution prints:

/tag/staff-picks/   tag-staff-picks > tag > archive > index
                    => archive
/author/devon/      author-devon > author > archive > index
                    => archive
/hello-world/       single-post-hello-world > single-post > single > singular > index
                    => single
/?s=dune            search > index
                    => search
/                   front-page > home > index
                    => home
/contact/           page-contact > page > singular > index
                    => page

Accept any loop that stops at the first match. Without break the last match wins and every row says index: the most instructive bug of the lab. On a site where pagesco is active, / answers index (pagesco has no home template), which is the template map's "index doubles as home".

All three default themes are block themes; Twenty Twenty-Three has no functions.php at all, which shows that PHP is optional in a block theme. The pattern counts change whenever a default theme updates. grep -m 1 matters: some style.css files repeat a field in their description.

Lab 3.2-B: labs/module-04/l3_2-block-theme/theme_json_reader_starter.php → theme_json_reader_solution.php

The solution prints:

twentytwentyfive: theme.json version 3
content width: 645px  wide width: 1340px
palette: base, contrast, accent-1, accent-2, accent-3, accent-4, accent-5, accent-6
font sizes: small, medium, large, x-large, xx-large

The file holds the shorthand (var:preset|color|contrast); the page holds the custom property; wp_get_global_settings() / wp_get_global_styles() return the MERGED data (core, theme, the owner's Site Editor changes), which is what code should read in practice. ☕ Staff picks for the season
☕ Welcome to the new Pages &#038; Co. website

Clean up: switch back to your theme and wp theme delete pagesco-cafe. The lesson in it: a block theme fails quietly; the messages that do exist appear only with WP_DEBUG on.

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 3.1 · Theme Anatomy & the Template Hierarchy
  1. A singular.html: The page chain is page-about, page-12, page, singular, index. The theme has no page template, so the first match is singular.html.
  2. C A hybrid: PHP templates plus a theme.json: No templates/index.html, so it is not a block theme, but it has a theme.json: a classic theme with block features, a hybrid.
  3. B page.html: There is no front-page.html, and with a static front page WordPress follows the page chain next, so page.html draws it.
  4. C Her save stored a copy as a wp_template post, and that copy wins over the file: Saving in the Site Editor stores a wp_template post with the source custom, and that copy wins over the theme file until it is reset.
  5. B add_filter( 'single_template_hierarchy', ... ) returning a changed array: single_template_hierarchy is the filter WordPress applies to the candidate list for single posts; a callback receives the array and returns the list to search.
Lesson 3.2 · Building a Block Theme
  1. B --wp--preset--color--oxblood: Every palette color becomes --wp--preset--color--SLUG in the global styles, which is what var:preset|color|oxblood stands for.
  2. A style.css and templates/index.html: style.css carries the theme header and templates/index.html is the template every request can fall back to. theme.json and functions.php are optional.
  3. C The page without a footer, and no message: The Template Part block finds no part with that slug and renders nothing. Only logged-in users see a hint, so check slugs while logged in.
  4. A In settings.color: the palette, plus custom set to false: What the editors offer is a setting: settings.color.palette lists the colors and settings.color.custom set to false removes the free picker.
  5. B Decide which version is right, then reset or delete the saved copy (exporting it first if it should be kept): The post is a Site Editor copy that wins over the file. Reset it in the Site Editor or delete it with wp post delete ID --force, after exporting it if the owner's change should be kept.

Exit Ticket

  1. archive.html. The candidates are author-maria, author-2, author, archive, index; the theme has none of the first three, so archive answers. (index would answer only without archive.)
  2. Palette: settings.color.palette (what editors may choose from). Background: styles.color.background, written var:preset|color|paper so it follows the preset.
  3. Two of: a template-part slug that matches no file in parts/ (silent for visitors; logged in you see "Template part has been deleted or is unavailable"); a Site Editor copy (wp_template or wp_template_part post) that wins over the file. A JSON syntax error removes the styles, not the header.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 C (single-page-...). Applies the post chain to pages. Pages have their own chain; show the demo's log for /about/.
  • Q3 A (a warning). Expects PHP to complain about a hook nobody fires. A hook that never fires is silent by design.
  • Q5 A (home.html). The most common hierarchy mistake of all: home is the latest-posts list. front-page comes first.
  • Q8 B (1.75rem). Thinks presets are copied in. They are referenced: that is what makes style variations possible (Lesson 3.3).
  • Q10 B or C. Confuses the course site (WP_DEBUG on) with a live site. On a live site the design just goes plain.
  • Q12 D. Has not looked at the preset grep: black and white are always printed.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 5: Patterns, Style Variations and Child Themes

Answers and reasoning for every Session 5 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-run items, the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 5 12 self-check items Confidential

Start Here · Warm-up

Session 4 retrieval, not graded. W5's output on a site ready for today:

wp eval 'var_dump( wp_is_block_theme(), wp_theme_has_theme_json() );'
wp post list --post_type=wp_template,wp_template_part,wp_global_styles --format=count
bool(true)
bool(true)
0
  1. W1 In theme.json, what is the difference between settings and styles? Answer: settings say what editors may choose (the palette, font sizes, layout widths); styles say what is applied (the page's colors, fonts, element and block styles). Presets in settings become custom properties and classes (Lesson 3.2).
  2. W2 The front page shows the latest posts. In a block theme, which templates does WordPress look for, in order? Answer: front-page, then home, then index (Lesson 3.1). pagesco has only index.html so far; Lesson 3.3 adds front-page.html today.
  3. W3 The manager changed the Single Posts template in the Site Editor. Where is that change, and which version does a visitor see? Answer: In the database, a wp_template post whose source is custom; it wins over templates/single.html until someone resets it (Lesson 3.2, Common Mistakes 3).
  4. W4 A template part is included as {"slug":"heder"}. What do visitors see? And a logged-in administrator? Answer: Visitors: nothing, the header is just missing. Logged in: "Template part has been deleted or is unavailable: heder" (Lesson 3.2).
  5. W5 Run the two commands on the slide. What should a site ready for today print? Answer: bool(true) twice (pagesco is a block theme with a theme.json), then 0: no Site Editor copies. A learner with copies resets them first (wp post delete ID --force), or today's outputs will not match.

3.3 · Patterns, Style Variations & the Site Editor: self-check

Q1
The live site has no WP_DEVELOPMENT_MODE. You deploy pagesco with a new patterns/gift-cards.php, and the inserter does not show it for half an hour. What makes WordPress read the patterns folder again at once, as part of every release?
A) Raise the theme's Version in style.css
B) Call register_block_pattern() for the file in functions.php
C) Run wp cache flush
D) Rename patterns/ and back
Why: The list of pattern files is cached in a transient (wp_theme_files_patterns-…) for 30 minutes, and rebuilt early when the theme's Version changes or the theme is switched (Lesson 3.3). Bumping Version on every deploy is the habit Lesson 8.4 builds in; the transient holds the version it was built for.
B is wrong: Files in patterns/ register themselves; registering the same pattern by hand is a second, different mechanism.
C is wrong: Without a persistent object cache, transients live in the options table, which a cache flush does not touch.
D is wrong: The cache is keyed on the theme, not on the folder's name; renaming does nothing reliable.
Q2
On the site at the end of Lesson 3.3, what does this print, and what are the two numbers?
wp eval 'echo count( WP_Theme_JSON_Resolver::get_style_variations() ), " ", count( WP_Theme_JSON_Resolver::get_style_variations( "block" ) ), "\n";'
1 2: one site variation (Evening) and two section styles (Sage band, Ink band)
Why: The resolver sorts every JSON file under styles/ by one key: a file with blockTypes is a section style (the second call), a file without it a style variation for the whole site (the first). The folder styles/sections/ is only for order.
Q3
Which of these lines should never be written into a template file in a theme?
A) <!-- wp:pattern {"slug":"pagesco/hero"} /-->
B) <!-- wp:template-part {"slug":"footer"} /-->
C) <!-- wp:block {"ref":20} /-->
D) <!-- wp:query {"query":{"inherit":true}} -->
Why: A synced pattern is referenced by its post ID, and IDs differ on every site: on another copy of the site, post 20 is something else or nothing. Themes refer to patterns by slug and to parts by slug; synced patterns are the site owner's content (Lesson 3.3, "Synced patterns are content, not theme").
A is wrong: A theme pattern by slug is exactly how templates use patterns.
B is wrong: Template parts are included by slug from parts/.
D is wrong: The Query Loop that inherits the main query is how lists are drawn in block templates.
Q4
After the shop manager saved the front page in the Site Editor, the wp_template copy contains "patternName":"pagesco/hero" and src="http://localhost:8080/...". What does that tell you?
A) The copy still refers to hero.php, so changes to the file will show up
B) The Site Editor could not read the pattern file and left a placeholder
C) Nothing: patternName is only used for the inserter's preview
D) The pattern was expanded before saving: its PHP already ran, so the URL is hard-coded and the strings are frozen in English
Why: When someone edits a template that uses wp:pattern, the editor replaces each reference with the pattern's blocks and records where they came from in metadata.patternName. Copying that template over the file would lose translation, theme URLs and escaping; move the actual change into the pattern file and reset the copy.
A is wrong: No wp:pattern reference is left in the copy; it is ordinary blocks now.
B is wrong: The content is complete; the metadata is a note, not a placeholder.
C is wrong: It is a record of where the blocks came from; the important part is that the reference is gone.
Q5
You move section-ink.json from styles/sections/ up into styles/. What changes?
A) It becomes a style variation for the whole site
B) Nothing: it is still a section style, because it has blockTypes
C) WordPress ignores it: section styles must be in styles/sections/
D) It replaces evening.json, because both are in styles/ now
Why: WordPress reads every JSON file under styles/, at any depth, and tells the two kinds apart by the blockTypes key alone. The subfolder exists only for people.
A is wrong: A file becomes a site variation only when it has no blockTypes.
C is wrong: There is no required folder name.
D is wrong: Files replace each other by name only in a child theme (Lesson 3.4), and the names differ.
Q6
The page source announces Vollkorn in a @font-face rule, but every browser shows the fallback serif, and debug.log is empty. Where do you look first, and for what?
The browser's developer tools, Network tab filtered by Font: the font request answers 404, so the src path after file:./ (or the folder name) is wrong
Why: WordPress builds the @font-face URL from theme.json without checking that the file exists (Lesson 3.3, Common Mistakes 2). From the WP-CLI container, curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}' <the font URL> shows the same 404.

3.3 · Patterns, Style Variations & the Site Editor: predict, then run

P1
Predict the output, then run it.
wp eval 'var_dump( WP_Block_Patterns_Registry::get_instance()->get_registered( "pagesco/hero" )["postTypes"] );'
array(1) {
  [0]=>
  string(11) "wp_template"
}
Why: The header line Post Types: wp_template becomes an array with one entry: WordPress offers the hero only when editing templates in the Site Editor, never in a page (where its <h1> would compete with the page title).
P2
Predict the output, then run it.
curl -s http://localhost:8080/about/ | grep -c 'wp:block'
0
Why: Nothing of the reference reaches the browser: the page stores <!-- wp:block {"ref":20} /-->, and WordPress renders the synced pattern's blocks in its place. grep -c counts 0 and exits with status 1. The same holds for wp:pattern in templates.

3.4 · Child Themes & Customizing Existing Themes: self-check

Q7
A client uploads only your child theme (pagesco-tt5-child.zip) to a site where Twenty Twenty-Five has been deleted, and clicks Activate. What happens?
A) WordPress uses its default theme as the parent instead
B) It refuses: the parent theme is missing, and the theme is listed under Broken Themes
C) The child activates, unstyled, until the parent is installed
D) WordPress downloads Twenty Twenty-Five automatically
Why: A child is only meaningful on top of its parent. WP-CLI says Error: The parent theme is missing. Please install the "twentytwentyfive" parent theme.; the Themes screen lists the child under Broken Themes (Lesson 3.4, Common Mistakes 1, where a typo in Template: caused the same). Install the parent first.
A is wrong: Template: names one folder; WordPress never guesses another parent.
C is wrong: Without a parent there are no templates to draw a page with; activation is refused.
D is wrong: Nothing is downloaded on activation; wp theme install or the Themes screen does that.
Q8
On the site at the end of Lesson 3.4: what do the two middle commands print?
wp theme activate pagesco-tt5-child
wp option get stylesheet
wp option get template
wp theme activate pagesco
pagesco-tt5-child, then twentytwentyfive
Why: With a child theme active, the stylesheet option holds the child (the theme whose style.css header names the site's theme) and the template option holds the parent (where the templates and code come from). Without a child theme both hold the same folder name. The first and last lines switch the child on and back to pagesco.
Q9
A colleague's child theme.json lists only the two colors they wanted to change, base and contrast. What happens to Twenty Twenty-Five's accent colors?
A) They disappear: the child's palette replaces the parent's whole palette, and styles that use accent variables silently lose their colors
B) They stay: theme.json is merged key by key
C) WordPress prints a notice that the palette is incomplete
D) The child's two colors are added to the end of the parent's list
Why: Single values merge key by key, but preset lists (palette, font sizes, spacing sizes) are replaced as a whole. The home page still uses var(--wp--preset--color--accent-3) and accent-4, now undefined, and CSS ignores an undefined variable without a message (Lesson 3.4, Common Mistakes 2). Repeat every slug, change only values.
B is wrong: That is true for single values, not for preset lists.
C is wrong: There is no message anywhere; that is what makes it dangerous.
D is wrong: Lists are not appended; the child's list is the list.
Q10
Why can a child of Twenty Twenty-Five replace twentytwentyfive_post_format_setup() by defining a function with that name in its own functions.php?
A) Child functions always override parent functions of the same name
B) WordPress renames the parent's function when a child theme is active
C) The parent's function is registered on a hook, and hooks prefer the child
D) The child's functions.php loads first, and the parent defines that function only if ( ! function_exists( ... ) )
Why: Load order (Lesson 3.4: child, then parent) plus a pluggable function. For a parent function WITHOUT the function_exists() wrapper, defining the same name in the child is a fatal Cannot redeclare error; remove or replace its hook instead, on after_setup_theme.
A is wrong: PHP does not allow two functions with one name; only the wrapper makes it possible.
B is wrong: WordPress renames nothing.
C is wrong: Hooks have priorities, not a preference for the child; and the replacement here is a function definition.
Q11
Twenty Twenty-Four enqueues no stylesheet of its own. In a child of it you write wp_enqueue_style( 'my-child-style', get_stylesheet_uri(), $deps, $version ). What should $deps be?
A) array( 'twentytwentyfour-style' )
B) array( 'wp-block-library' )
C) array()
D) get_template_directory_uri() . '/style.css'
Why: There is no parent handle to wait for, so the child depends on nothing (the session's TT4 child lab does exactly this). Read the parent's functions.php before copying a tutorial's handle: on WordPress 7.1 a dependency that is not registered stops the stylesheet from printing at all, with a notice that it was enqueued with dependencies that are not registered (since 6.9.1).
A is wrong: That handle does not exist: WordPress drops the child's stylesheet and notes it in debug.log.
B is wrong: The child's CSS has nothing to do with the block library's; the dependency only delays it for no reason.
D is wrong: That is a URL, not a list of handles, and it points at the parent's style.css header file.
Q12
The shop manager wants to try a darker link color for the holidays and switch it back in January, without calling you. Site Editor, child theme, or plugin? Why?
The Site Editor's Styles: a visual choice the owner wants to change again; it is saved in the database (the user global styles) and needs no developer
Why: The decision table of Lesson 3.4: who should change it later, and must it survive a theme switch? Lasting design work on a theme you did not write goes in a child theme, kept in Git; features go in plugins; owner-changeable looks belong to the Site Editor.

3.4 · Child Themes & Customizing Existing Themes: predict, then run

P3
Predict the output, then run it.
wp theme activate pagesco-tt5-child
wp eval 'echo get_theme_file_uri( "assets/images/book-image.webp" ), "\n";'
wp theme activate pagesco
Success: Switched to 'Pages &amp; Co. TT5 Child' theme.
http://localhost:8080/wp-content/themes/twentytwentyfive/assets/images/book-image.webp
Success: Switched to 'Pages &amp; Co.' theme.
Why: The child has no assets/ folder, so get_theme_file_uri() falls back to the parent's copy, and Twenty Twenty-Five happens to ship a book-image.webp of its own. That lookup is why the hero uses get_theme_file_uri(): a child of pagesco could replace the image by adding its own file.
P4
Predict the output, then run it.
wp theme activate pagesco-tt5-child
wp eval 'var_dump( function_exists( "twentytwentyfive_post_format_setup" ), get_theme_support( "post-formats" )[0] );'
wp theme activate pagesco
Success: Switched to 'Pages &amp; Co. TT5 Child' theme.
bool(true)
array(2) {
  [0]=>
  string(5) "aside"
  [1]=>
  string(5) "quote"
}
Success: Switched to 'Pages &amp; Co.' theme.
Why: function_exists() is true, and the post formats are the child's two: the child's functions.php loaded first and defined twentytwentyfive_post_format_setup(), so the parent skipped its own definition (a pluggable function).

Lab Reference Solutions

Every output below is real: the lab files run exactly as labs/check_labs.py runs them, on a course site at the state each file declares (WordPress 7.1.3, PHP 8.5, MySQL 9.7, WP-CLI 2.12). Folder labs are installed as a theme and activated first (the first line of their output); paths show /var/www/html/..., the learner's wordpress/ folder.

Lab 3.3-A (guided): labs/module-05/l3_3-patterns-style-variations/hours_band_starter/ → hours_band_solution/ with test_hours_band.php

The test with the starter's pattern:

Success: Switched to 'Pages &amp; Co. Lab: Hours Band' theme.
Title:       Visit the shop: hours band
Categories:  (none)
Keywords:    (none)
Description: (none)
Heading:     Visit the shop
Text:        12 Market Street, open late on Thursdays.
Button:      Plan your visit -> http://localhost:8080/contact/

With the solution's:

Success: Switched to 'Pages &amp; Co. Lab: Hours Band' theme.
Title:       Visit the shop: hours band
Categories:  contact
Keywords:    hours, address, visit
Description: A sage band with the shop's address and a button to the Contact page.
Heading:     VISIT THE SHOP
Text:        12 MARKET STREET, OPEN LATE ON THURSDAYS.
Button:      PLAN YOUR VISIT -> https://pagesandco.example/contact/

Reading it: the header fields arrive in the registry as arrays (Categories, Keywords) or strings; the content is the pattern file's output, produced the first time someone asks for it, which is why the test's two filters are in place in time. Lower case after the "translation" means a string without esc_html_e() in the child's text domain (pagesco would not change case: another theme's strings); localhost means a hard-coded URL. Common sticking points: esc_html_e() inside echo (it already prints), a missing esc_url() (the test cannot see it; ask for it anyway), Categories: Contact with a capital (category names are slugs).

Lab 3.3-B: labs/module-05/l3_3-patterns-style-variations/variation_contrast_starter.php → variation_contrast_solution.php

As shipped:

Default   ink on paper      TODO
Default   oxblood on paper  TODO
Default   slate on paper    TODO

The solution prints:

Default   ink on paper      13.9:1  AA
Default   oxblood on paper   8.8:1  AA
Default   slate on paper     5.8:1  AA
Default   ink on sage       12.0:1  AA
Default   sage on paper      1.2:1  FAIL (needs 4.5)
Evening   ink on paper      13.5:1  AA
Evening   oxblood on paper   8.3:1  AA
Evening   slate on paper     8.4:1  AA
Evening   ink on sage        9.7:1  AA
Evening   sage on paper      1.4:1  FAIL (needs 4.5)

The numbers confirm the lesson (Evening: ink on paper 13.5:1, oxblood links 8.3:1) and add one it does not state: sage on paper is 1.2:1, which is why sage is a background color only (Lesson 3.2's handoff calls it that). The two traps: the variation's palette is in ['settings']['color']['palette']['theme'] (keyed by origin, the resolver's internal shape); and a variation is a LAYER, so its colors are merged over the theme's (array_merge()), exactly as WordPress merges them.

Handout: labs/module-05/l3_3-patterns-style-variations/debug_pattern_files.sh (deliberately broken)

As shipped:

== what WordPress registered ==
Notice: Function get_block_patterns was called <strong>incorrectly</strong>. Could not register file "/var/www/html/wp-content/themes/pagesco/patterns/gift-cards.php" as a block pattern ("Slug" field missing) Please see <a href="https://developer.wordpress.org/advanced-administration/debug/debug-wordpress/">Debugging in WordPress</a> for more information. (This message was added in version 6.0.0.) in /var/www/html/wp-includes/functions.php on line 6260
pattern: pagesco/call-to-action
pattern: pagesco/featured-books
pattern: pagesco/hero
Notice: wp_json_file_decode(): Error when decoding a JSON file at path /var/www/html/wp-content/themes/pagesco/styles/sepia.json: Syntax error in /var/www/html/wp-includes/functions.php on line 6260
site variation: Evening
site variation: sepia
section style: Ink band
section style: Sage band
  1. doing_it_wrong notice, the pattern header (script line 22): no Slug: line, so WordPress skips the file ("Slug" field missing). Add * Slug: pagesco/gift-cards.
  2. Notice, script line 45 (sepia.json): a trailing comma after the last palette entry: wp_json_file_decode() reports a syntax error, and the file is still listed, under its FILE NAME (sepia) with no data. Remove the comma.
  3. Silent, script line 40 (sepia.json): "blockTypes" copied from a section style. With the comma fixed, Sepia is registered as a section style for Groups, not as a look for the whole site. Delete the line. (Bug 2 hides bug 3: say so when a learner "fixes" the comma and asks why Sepia is still not a site variation.)

Fixed, the script prints:

pattern: pagesco/call-to-action
pattern: pagesco/featured-books
pattern: pagesco/gift-cards
pattern: pagesco/hero
site variation: Evening
site variation: Sepia
section style: Ink band
section style: Sage band
Lab 3.4-A (guided): labs/module-05/l3_4-child-themes/tt4_child_starter/ → tt4_child_solution/ with test_tt4_child.sh

The test with the starter:

Success: Switched to 'Pages &amp; Co. Lab: TT4 Child' theme.
template:    twentytwentyfour
stylesheet:  pagesco-lab-tt4-child
accent-3:    #d8613c
palette:     10 theme colors
child CSS:   0
footer:      (not the shop's: still the parent's footer pattern)
styles:      core/details arrow-icon-details, core/post-terms pill, core/list checkmark-list, core/navigation-link arrow-link, core/heading asterisk

With the solution:

Success: Switched to 'Pages &amp; Co. Lab: TT4 Child' theme.
template:    twentytwentyfour
stylesheet:  pagesco-lab-tt4-child
accent-3:    #7a2e2e
palette:     10 theme colors
child CSS:   1
footer:      The reading corner at Pages &amp; Co. · 12 Market Street
styles:      core/list checkmark-list

Reading it, line by line: template/stylesheet (the starter is already a working child); accent-3 is the child's only when the child's palette lists it, and 10 theme colors stays 10 only when the child repeats every slug (a palette with one entry makes it 1); the child's CSS is loaded once enqueued, with array() as dependencies because Twenty Twenty-Four registers no stylesheet handle of its own (a copied twentytwentyfour-style makes WordPress drop the child's stylesheet and log that the dependency is not registered); the footer is the child's once the pattern has the parent's slug; and the pluggable twentytwentyfour_block_styles() replaced the parent's five styles with one.

Lab 3.4-B: labs/module-05/l3_4-child-themes/merge_layers_starter.php → merge_layers_solution.php

As shipped:

parent:  8 colors, accent-3 = #503AA8
child:   TODO
merged:  TODO
link:    TODO
body:    TODO
h1 size: TODO
partial: TODO

The solution prints:

parent:  8 colors, accent-3 = #503AA8
child:   8 colors, accent-3 = #7a2e2e
merged:  8 colors, accent-3 = #7a2e2e
link:    var(--wp--preset--color--accent-3)  (from the child)
body:    var(--wp--preset--font-family--manrope)  (from the parent)
h1 size: var(--wp--preset--font-size--xx-large)  (from the parent)
partial: 2 colors (base, contrast)

The parent's accent-3 (#503AA8) is replaced by the child's oxblood; the link color comes from the child, the body font and the h1 size from the parent: the merge rule of Lesson 3.4 in four lines. Note that WP_Theme_JSON turned the files' var:preset|color|accent-3 shorthand into the CSS custom property it prints. The partial palette keeps two colors: lists are replaced, never merged.

  1. Notice, script line 39 (theme.json): a trailing comma, so wp_json_file_decode() fails and the child's whole theme.json is ignored: the link color is the parent's currentColor. Remove the comma.
  2. doing_it_wrong notice, script line 54 (functions.php): wp_enqueue_style() at the top level, before wp_enqueue_scripts ("Scripts and styles should not be registered or enqueued until..."). The stylesheet still loads, which is why people ignore it; wrap it in a function on wp_enqueue_scripts. The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 3.3 · Patterns, Style Variations & the Site Editor
  1. B The cached list of the theme's pattern files is still the old one: WordPress caches the list of pattern files per theme version. Set WP_DEVELOPMENT_MODE to theme on a development site, or raise the theme Version when you deploy.
  2. C A synced pattern: A synced pattern is one wp_block post; every page references it, so editing it once updates all six.
  3. A One with "blockTypes": [ "core/group" ] and a styles section: The blockTypes key is what makes a file in styles/ a block style variation (section style) instead of a style variation for the whole site.
  4. B Find the actual change, put it into the right theme file (often a pattern), then reset the copy: The export has the patterns expanded, with hard-coded URLs and frozen text. Move the actual change into the file it belongs to, usually a pattern, then reset the database copy.
  5. C Check whether its license allows web use and redistribution in a theme: Desktop or print licenses usually forbid web embedding and redistribution. Check the license (or buy a web license); until then use an OFL font or a system stack.
Lesson 3.4 · Child Themes & Customizing Existing Themes
  1. B twentytwentyfive: template holds the parent's folder (twentytwentyfive); stylesheet holds the child's. Without a child theme both are the same.
  2. A It stays: the child's file is merged over the parent's: Single values in theme.json are merged key by key, so everything the child does not set keeps the parent's value. Only preset lists such as the palette are replaced as a whole.
  3. C false, and the parent's stylesheet is still loaded: The child's functions.php runs before the parent's, so the action has not been added yet and there is nothing to remove. Do it from a callback on after_setup_theme.
  4. B A child pattern with the slug twentytwentyfive/footer: The parent's footer part only references the pattern twentytwentyfive/footer, and a child pattern with the same slug wins, so the part itself stays the parent's.
  5. C In a plugin: A form is a feature with data and security needs; it must survive a theme switch, so it belongs in a plugin (Module 5).

Exit Ticket

  1. Opening hours on four pages: a synced pattern, edited once, shown everywhere it is referenced. A hero for new landing pages: a theme pattern, a starting point that is copied and then edited per page.
  2. Site Editor storage: wp_template (and wp_template_part) posts for templates and parts, the wp_global_styles post for styles; wp post list --post_type=wp_template,wp_template_part,wp_global_styles lists them. (Synced patterns are wp_block posts, created on purpose.)
  3. Load order: the child's functions.php first, then the parent's. A parent hook does not exist yet when the child's file runs, so remove it in a callback on after_setup_theme; a pluggable parent function can be replaced by defining it first.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 B (register_block_pattern). Thinks pattern files need registering. Show the registry before and after development mode: no code, only the cache.
  • Q3 A or B. Thinks every block comment is risky in a template. The question is what the comment refers to: a slug travels, an ID does not.
  • Q4 A. The most important miss of the session: the learner believes the copy still points at hero.php. Rerun the demo's grep on the copy.
  • Q5 A. Believes the folder decides. Point at the resolver's one key: blockTypes.
  • Q9 B. Knows "merged" from the lesson and applies it to lists. Run the partial-palette count.
  • Q10 A. "Child wins" as a general rule. Ask what PHP does with two functions of the same name.
  • Q11 A. Copied the lesson's handle. The TT4 lab is the cure: read the parent's functions.php.

Session 6: The Loop and Custom Queries

Answers and reasoning for every Session 6 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-run items, the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 6 12 self-check items Confidential

Start Here · Warm-up

Session 5 retrieval, not graded. W5's output on a site ready for today:

wp theme list --fields=name,status
wp post list --post_type=wp_template,wp_template_part,wp_global_styles --format=count
+-------------------+----------+
| name              | status   |
+-------------------+----------+
| pagesco-tt5-child | inactive |
| pagesco           | active   |
| twentytwentyfive  | inactive |
| twentytwentyfour  | inactive |
| twentytwentythree | inactive |
+-------------------+----------+
0
  1. W1 Theme pattern or synced pattern: which one is a post in the database, and why does a theme never ship one? Answer: The synced pattern: a wp_block post referenced by ID, and IDs differ on every site (Lesson 3.3).
  2. W2 Two JSON files in styles/: what makes one a section style and the other a style variation? Answer: The blockTypes key: with it, a section style for the listed blocks; without it, a look for the whole site.
  3. W3 A child theme is active. What do the template and stylesheet options hold? Answer: template = the parent's folder, stylesheet = the child's (Lesson 3.4).
  4. W4 Why did the child's style.css not load until functions.php enqueued it, and why must a parent hook be removed on after_setup_theme? Answer: A block theme's style.css is a header file; CSS loads only when enqueued. The child's functions.php runs first, so the parent's hooks do not exist yet when it runs.
  5. W5 Run the two commands on the slide. What should a site ready for today print? Answer: pagesco active, pagesco-tt5-child inactive (Twenty Twenty-One deleted), and 0 Site Editor copies. Today's demo starts by building pagesco-classic and switching to it.

4.1 · The Loop & Template Tags: self-check

Q1
A classic theme has only index.php, singular.php and 404.php. Which file draws /author/maria/?
A) singular.php
B) index.php, with a Loop over Maria's posts
C) author.php, which WordPress generates
D) 404.php, because there is no author template
Why: The hierarchy for an author archive is author-{nicename}, author-{id}, author, archive, then index (Lesson 3.1). With none of the specific files, index.php runs, and its Loop walks whatever the main query found: Maria's two posts. That is why one index.php can draw every list.
A is wrong: singular.php is for one post or page.
C is wrong: WordPress never generates templates.
D is wrong: 404 is only for requests whose main query finds nothing at all.
Q2
On the course site, what does this print, and why?
wp eval 'var_dump( is_home(), get_the_title() );'
bool(false) and string(0) "": WP-CLI loads WordPress but runs no main query, so is_home() has nothing to answer from and there is no current post for get_the_title()
Why: Conditional tags and template tags read the main query and the global $post, which only a front-end request (or wp(), as the session's debug handouts do) fills. In a template both would work.
Q3
Inside the Loop you need the post's title in a link's title attribute and as the link text inside an h2. Which pair is right?
A) title="<?php the_title(); ?>" and echo '<h2>' . the_title() . '</h2>';
B) title="<?php echo get_the_title(); ?>" and the_title( '<h2>', '</h2>' );
C) title="<?php echo esc_html( the_title() ); ?>" and the_title();
D) title="<?php the_title_attribute(); ?>" and the_title( '<h2>', '</h2>' );
Why: the_title() prints text prepared for HTML, not for an attribute; the_title_attribute() (or esc_attr( get_the_title() )) is the attribute version. the_title() with $before and $after wraps itself; concatenating it into a string prints the title first and an empty h2 after it (Lesson 4.1, Common Mistakes 1).
A is wrong: Both halves are wrong: an unescaped attribute, and the concatenation mistake.
B is wrong: The h2 is right; the attribute is not escaped for its context.
C is wrong: the_title() prints and returns nothing, so esc_html() escapes an empty value after the title is already out.
Q4
get_template_part( 'template-parts/content.php' ); prints nothing, and there is no message. Why?
A) It looked for template-parts/content.php.php, found nothing and returned false
B) Template parts only work inside the Loop
C) Template parts must be in the theme's root folder
D) It found the file but needs a third argument with the post
Why: get_template_part() adds .php itself and goes through locate_template() (child first, then parent). A missing file is not an error: the function returns false, a found file null. var_dump() its return value when a part seems to print nothing.
B is wrong: Parts can run anywhere; content.php just uses template tags, which need a current post.
C is wrong: Any path relative to the theme works.
D is wrong: $args is optional (WordPress 5.5+).
Q5
A plugin must do something only on single posts and pages. Which is the EARLIEST of these hooks on which is_singular() gives the right answer?
A) plugins_loaded
B) init
C) template_redirect
D) wp_footer
Why: Conditional tags read the main query, which runs after init (wp() in the request order, Lesson 2.2). template_redirect fires after the query and before the template, which is why the lesson's conditions tool logs there. wp_footer works too, but much later.
A is wrong: Before the query: the tag returns false, with a "called incorrectly" notice.
B is wrong: Still before the query.
D is wrong: It works, but it is not the earliest; by then the page is printed.
Q6
singular.php shows only one page. Why does it still use while ( have_posts() ) { the_post(); ... }?
The main query holds exactly one post, and the_post() is what sets the global $post that every template tag reads (and fires loop_start / loop_end for plugins)
Why: On a single post or page the Loop has one item. Skipping it leaves template tags without a current post, and plugins that hook into the Loop never see it.

4.1 · The Loop & Template Tags: predict, then run

P1
Predict the output, then run it.
curl -s http://localhost:8080/author/devon/ | grep -o '<h1>.*</h1>' | sed 's/<[^>]*>//g'
Author: Devon Park
Why: No author branch in index.php: the_archive_title() knows every archive type and prints "Author: Devon Park" (the tags removed by sed). One call covers category, tag, author and date archives.
P2
Predict the output, then run it.
wp eval 'var_dump( did_action( "wp" ), have_posts() );'
int(0)
bool(false)
Why: WP-CLI loads WordPress but never runs wp(), so the wp action has fired 0 times and the main query has no posts: have_posts() is false. That is why the session's debug handouts call wp( 's=dune' ) themselves.

4.2 · Custom Queries: self-check

Q7
On the site at the end of Lesson 4.1: what does this print, and why the second number?
wp eval '$q = new WP_Query( array( "post_type" => "page", "posts_per_page" => 2, "no_found_rows" => true ) ); echo $q->post_count, " ", $q->found_posts, "\n";'
2 0: two pages loaded; found_posts is 0 because no_found_rows skips the counting query
Why: no_found_rows is right for a list you will not paginate (Lesson 4.2's query-args demo). Without it found_posts would be the four published pages.
Q8
A pre_get_posts callback reads if ( is_home() ) { $query->set( 'posts_per_page', 3 ); } and nothing else. What goes wrong?
A) Nothing: is_home() makes sure only the blog home changes
B) Every other WP_Query that runs on the blog home (a box, a widget, a block) is also cut to three posts
C) It is a filter, so it must return $query
D) The admin's Posts screen breaks, but the front end is fine
Why: The callback runs for every WP_Query. The global is_home() describes the request, not the query being built, so secondary queries on the blog home pass the test too. Guard with is_admin(), $query->is_main_query() and the query's own $query->is_home() (Lesson 4.2).
A is wrong: It checks the request, not which query is about to run.
C is wrong: pre_get_posts is an action: the object is changed in place.
D is wrong: is_home() is false in the admin, so the admin is not the problem here.
Q9
A query with 'posts_per_page' => 3 matches 7 posts. On page 3, what are post_count, found_posts and max_num_pages?
A) 1, 7, 3
B) 3, 7, 3
C) 1, 3, 7
D) 3, 3, 3
Why: post_count is what this page holds (7 = 3 + 3 + 1), found_posts the total match, max_num_pages ceil(7 / 3). The session's query_shelf lab prints all three for every query.
B is wrong: Page 3 holds only the remainder.
C is wrong: found_posts and max_num_pages are swapped.
D is wrong: found_posts is the total, not the page size.
Q10
In which case does your code need NO wp_reset_postdata()?
A) After a WP_Query Loop at the end of a template part
B) After a secondary Loop inside the main Loop of singular.php
C) After a secondary Loop in front-page.php, before the main Loop
D) After get_posts(), when each WP_Post is passed to the functions you call (get_the_title( $post ))
Why: Only $query->the_post() (and setup_postdata()) replaces the global $post. get_posts() returns an array and leaves the global alone, as long as you pass each post along instead of calling setup_postdata() (Lesson 4.2, "get_posts() needs no reset").
A is wrong: Somebody will add a template tag after your part one day; reset.
B is wrong: That is exactly the broken post navigation of Common Mistakes 2.
C is wrong: The main Loop's first the_post() sets the post again, but tags between the two Loops would talk about the wrong post: reset anyway.
Q11
An old template starts with query_posts( 'cat=2&posts_per_page=5' );. How do you translate it?
A) Keep it, and call wp_reset_query() at the end
B) Replace it with get_posts() and the same string
C) If it changes the page's main list: pre_get_posts on the main query (category_name, posts_per_page); if it adds a list: new WP_Query plus wp_reset_postdata()
D) Move it into functions.php so it runs earlier
Why: query_posts() throws the main query away and runs a second one, so pagination and conditional tags disagree with what is shown; its own documentation says not to use it (Lesson 4.2 FAQ).
A is wrong: The reset only cleans up after the damage.
B is wrong: get_posts() returns data; it does not change what the template's Loop shows.
D is wrong: query_posts() in functions.php runs before the main query exists and breaks more.
Q12
Module 5 brings a Book post type. The shop wants (a) a "three newest books" box under every post and (b) 12 books per page on /books/. Which tool for each?
(a) a secondary WP_Query (or get_posts()) with posts_per_page 3, plus wp_reset_postdata() after a Loop; (b) pre_get_posts on the main query (is_post_type_archive( 'book' ), posts_per_page 12)
Why: Extra lists are secondary queries; the page's own list is the main query, changed before it runs so that pagination and the 404 decision agree (the lesson's decision table and journal prompt).

4.2 · Custom Queries: predict, then run

P3
Predict the output, then run it.
wp eval '$q = new WP_Query( array( "category_name" => "news", "posts_per_page" => 2 ) ); echo $q->post_count, " ", $q->found_posts, " ", $q->max_num_pages, "\n";'
2 3 2
Why: post_count 2 (this page), found_posts 3 (all News posts), max_num_pages 2 (ceil(3 / 2)).
P4
Predict the output, then run it.
curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n' http://localhost:8080/page/2/
curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n' http://localhost:8080/category/news/page/2/
200
404
Why: The home page's main query has three per page (pre_get_posts), so its page 2 exists; the News archive still has ten per page and three posts, so its page 2 does not, and WordPress answers 404 before any template runs.

The answers to the workbook's questions: post_title is stored with a plain &, the display filters (wptexturize) turn it into &#038;; the attribute version is the same here because the title has no tags or quotes; the raw content starts with a block comment, the rendered one with the paragraph's HTML (only the content filters render blocks); the excerpt was built from the content (no hand-written excerpt).
